Honeymoon Trail

Honeymoon Trail 

It was morning-morning for Ellen.

For hours the trucks had been wheezing and rattling along the street, the milk-cans had been banging on the curbstone, the elevated trains had been roaring past the windows; but those things were only unconsidered cock-crows to Ellen. Morning did not begin for her until the little alarm-clock in the kitchen shrilled seventhirty. At its first tingle she stirred and sighed; as it bored on with its persistent gimlet of sound she turned on her back and stretched daintily, luxuriously, reaching her arms above her head and her toes down to the foot of the cot; and when it stopped she sat up. She awoke without any of the groanings and noisy yawns with which most people orchestrate the beginning of their day; she had at all times a sensitive awareness that made even her unguarded actions fastidious. 

Ellen herself was as fresh as morning- as real morning in the dewy country; but the room she looked upon was like morning in its own neighborhood, shopworn and blowsy. In the blistered golden-oak bed which crowded her cot to the wall, her Aunt Lulie slept stertorously, a mound of discorded bedclothes and a

ΙΟ 

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

tousle of lace-trimmed boudoir-cap interspersed with frowsy wisps of hair. On the chair and floor adjacent lay sprawled Aunt Lulie's large garments; the bureau was mounted in humps and hillocks of neckwear, false puffs, soiled handkerchiefs, and the other myriad oddments of the untidy. The only orderly spots were the chair on which Ellen's slim near-silk garments lay neatly folded, and the narrow bed which even her sleeping had left well-tucked and seemly. The familiar scene gave her the familiar qualm of distaste, and she jumped up impatiently.

With that, the day was really begun; the fillip of dissatisfaction got her under way. 

When she had bathed and dressed with the fastidious care which was her only inheritance from her New England mother, she cooked cereal and made coffee and toast in the little kitchen; and having eaten her breakfast she gave the place the last tidying it would have that day. Then she put on her smart little hat and slender coat, and closed the door of the flat behind her. 

And now she was Ellen no longer; she was Geraldine.

Miss Geraldine James, moving steadily and purposefully up the world's escalator. 

When she emerged from the dingy doorway, she stood for a minute on the dingy steps, fastening her gloves and looking into the dingy street; and a shrill voice hailed her from the deeper dinginess of the area. "H'lo, Miss Geraldine! " 

Geraldine looked down, and saw, seated on an upturned soapbox, a pretty, dirty little girl, with a frock of undistinguishable hue and a chubby face overlaid like a palimpsest with past stickinesses. "Hello, Molly," said Geraldine, smiling at the cheer-

II ful grin that disclosed two rows of clean little white teeth in the midst of the surrounding grime. "You look awful pretty," remarked the dirty little girl, eyeing Geraldine's fresh face and dainty raiment approvingly. "You don't, Molly. You didn't do what I told you to, yesterday." 99 "Yes I did, Miss Geraldine. Honest. Kiss de cross." "No you didn't; I can see the same chocolate candy on your face the same banana, too. Why didn't you wash it?" "Didn't git no chanst. De sink was full o' dishes." "Then you must move some of them away. You've got such a nice, pretty face underneath, I want to see it. I don't like it one bit, the way it looks now." 

Molly's bright eyes lighted shrewdly. "I'll wash it good fer a nickle," she suggested. "All right, that's a bargain. Clean face, nickel; dirty face, no nickel. Promise!" "Kiss de cross!" returned the pious infant, cheerfully. 

Geraldine waved her hand and started away. The transaction seemed as reasonable to her as to the dirty little girl; she too thought it rather a waste of time to do an arduous and virtuous deed for nothing.

It was late September, on Columbus Avenue as well as in the country. Fewer melon-rinds and more decayed apples graced the ashcans along the curbstone; the delicatessen-dealers were taking the fly-netting off the cheeses. The air smelled of soot, of garbage, and oddly of morning freshness. Geraldine neither liked nor disliked it; she breathed it every day. She walked briskly and lightly to her subway station, descended 

---

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

into its cavernous depths on the surging tide of subwayfarers, and took her jostling, roaring, accustomed way to the office.

Arriving at the office had, until lately, given her a notable thrill. It was a long step up from her last office, as long a step as that had been from her home; a strictly high-class establishment, with the most modern equipment, the most considerate working conditions, and not a single overlord who called his stenographer "Cutie." She had been very proud when she entered this environment; in fact it was then that she had emerged from Ellen into Geraldine.

But already, after six months of it, the pressure of that inner urge which pushed her continually toward better things was growing uncomfortable. It was an excellent office, certainly; but is an office, however excellent, a place to spend one's entire life in? If she worked her hardest, for all her best years, she could rise no higher than Miss Heaton, the chief's middleaged secretary, who had grown gray-haired and grayfaced in the handling of other people's private affairs, and had no private affairs of her own to look forward to but a lonely old age. Not for Geraldine. Not good enough. She must go higher; but where, or how, she did not know.

Mr. Charlton's buzzer sounded as she was arranging her desk for the day, and she gathered up her implements and moved with her light, quick, purposeful step to his room. It had given her another notable thrill when she became stenographer to the head of the firm, but now even that was not enough. In fact it increased her undefined dissatisfaction: Mr. Charl ton and his office gave her a curious homesickness

for what? She herself wondered. re 

Good-morning, Miss James," said Mr. Charlton. "Nice day." 

Very nice, Mr. Charlton," answered Geraldine. "Take a letter, please," proceeded Mr. Charlton, as usual.

Geraldine, also as usual, studied him as she crossed her slim silken legs and poised her pencil. It was shrewd of her to see in Mr. Charlton the quality which made her homesick for the world she wanted, for he himself was completely without glamour-middle-aged, married and inclined to stoutness. But in his large clean face and pleasant voice, and in the quiet, unconscious courtesy of his manner, she discerned something of that rare atmosphere toward which she wistfully aspired. "Mr. John Leigh — L-e-i-g-h, P. O. Box 143, Craddocks' Falls, N. Y.," said Mr. Charlton, in the well-bred accent that so comforted her ear. ce ee

He paused, and glanced at Geraldine. "Are you discreet, Miss James?" he asked.

Outside her employer's presence she would have answered, "I'll tell the long-eared world I am!" for in matters of expression she inclined to the piquant and the forceful. But she was far too clever to talk out of character. She only let a very small smile deepen the

corners of her mouth and glint between her dark eyelashes, and said, as a perfectly discreet stenographer should, "Yes, sir." 

Mr. Charlton looked at her attentively not because she was very well worth looking at, but because it was his habit to make a careful appraisal of everyone in whom he had occasion to place confidence. "Yes," he said thoughtfully, "you're more than discreet; you're intelligent. All right, we'll go on.”

Geraldine felt a little glow of pleasure as she bent her dark head again over her pad. She liked to please 

Mr. Charlton, and she liked to succeed in her undertakings; as long as she was a stenographer, she wanted to be a good one. "To append a personal matter to a business note," resumed Mr. Charlton, "I have a message for you from

Geraldine's alert mind ran ahead of her nimble fingers, and while she swiftly and accurately translated

IS her employer's words into esoteric pot-hooks, she pictured the sort of person of whom one would request the favor of ten thousand dollars like asking for a hairpin. She knew the name of John Leigh, of course; no one whose education had been acquired mainly through the newspapers could be ignorant of it; but he did not figure in the social columns as many potential ten-thousand-dollar lenders did, and she had never seen a photograph of him. Probably he looked clean, middle-aged and portly, like Mr. Charlton. She wondered why millionaires were always old; surely, like all God's creatures, they must have a beginning. 

Bzzz! The drone of the desk telephone, subdued but challenging, cut into her reflections, and she stopped her quick pencil to answer it, for she was acting as Mr. Charlton's secretary while the austere Miss 

Heaton was on vacation. In a moment she turned away from the mouthpiece with a smile. ee "Mr. John Leigh's here right now, Mr. Charlton!' she said. ce دو 

Well, well! Angels, eh?" chuckled Mr. Charlton. "He's very apt to flap his wings unexpectedly. All right, Miss James, let him come in; and that'll be all for the present."

Geraldine picked up her paper and pad and moved demurely away, as a well-regulated piece of office machinery should do when no longer required. But as she neared the door she saw Mr. John Leigh approaching it from the other side, and, effacing herself against the wall, she stood back and took a good look at him. 

He did not look in the least like Mr. Charlton and the other millionaires she had seen about the office. 

He had no gray hairs, he had not so much as the pre-

- even unconsentiment of a convexity at the waist-line, there was no shortness of breath, and no suggestion scious of importance about him. He looked twentyeight or thirty, springy, vigorous, and somehow oddly boyish. His clothes were well made and carelessly worn, the clothes of a man who can afford to hang the expense and the appearances too. "Hello, Leigh!" exclaimed Mr. Charlton heartily. "Well, this north wind's a good wind after all!" ce 

Hello, Mr. Charlton!" answered John Leigh, in a vibrant voice that suggested vitality overlaid with shyness. "I had to come down to hurry up those concrete loafers, so I thought I'd collect the answer to my letter myself."

He was well inside the room now, and the two were shaking hands warmly. Geraldine had no excuse for lingering. She slipped out like a small, circumspect shadow, and Mr. John Leigh never saw her at all. 

She had seen Mr. John Leigh, though; she had never, in so short a time, seen anyone so thoroughly. She had seen his brown hair and his blue eyes with the thick brown brows and lashes, his short straight nose and his square cleft chin. She had seen the way he walked, with long, vigorous strides, and even for she had the sharp eyes of the self-dependent child of the city- the way he crushed his excellent soft hat under his arm as if it were a thing of no importance. Adjusted to a standard of pulchritude set by movie heroes, she did not think him handsome, but there was something extremely appealing about him even to her critical eye. 

As she remarked to herself, with her city-child worldliness, anybody with that much money couldn't help being good-looking, if you knew whom you were looking at.

When she returned to her desk she had nothing to do, for the superfluous letter to Mr. Leigh had been the first of the morning's crop; and, her energetic nature abhorring idleness, she opened a drawer and took out some needlework. She was copying, cleverly and successfully, a bit of neckwear she had seen in the window of an exclusive Fifth Avenue shop; her whole sophisticated little person was a testimony to her skill in imitation.

A snub-nosed, sandy-haired youth, in excessively modish raiment profusely ornamented with buttons, lounged across the room to seat himself on her desk. 

He answered to the name of Bunny Grimes, and was general-utility man about the office, where he easily managed to make his utility more general than special. "Howdy, Princess! How's the royal blood flowin' today?" he inquired facetiously. Like her, he had the city child's quick-eyed shrewdness; he was aware of her superior quality and of her innate ambition, and being a professed comedian he made capital of what he considered weaknesses. "A-one, thank you; how's God's little gift to the funnies?" Geraldine countered smartly. ee

When she arrived at Mr. Charlton's door, he was just opening it to let the visitor out. She was glad of this, for it gave her a minute more to observe him. She stood back against the wall, as she had done beforeeffaced, yet intensely present. "Don't you want to see Appleton yourself?" Mr.

His face lighted with a smile, boyish and unexpectedly vivid; the older man smiled back, with unmistakable affection; and the two clasped hands.

Geraldine dropped the letter in the intensity of her interest; but, even though he nearly tripped over her as she bent to pick it up, John Leigh went past her, as before, without seeing her at all.

Mr. Charlton moved forward to give the door a push, for he liked to work in quiet; but before he had closed it she slipped through. "Here's a letter marked 'Urgent,' Mr. Charlton," she said.

"Thank you, Miss James," he answered, taking it. "That's a remarkable young man who just went out.' "Is he, Mr. Charlton?" asked Geraldine, with respectful eagerness. "Yes. He's about the most sought-after person in 

New York, and about the loneliest."

She held her breath in the hope of more information, but Mr. Charlton had already opened the letter and closed the conversation. 

Geraldine went away slowly, thinking absorbedly about this remarkable young man and the world he lived in — the unknown world of beauty and graciousness. She thought of great, stately rooms, full of soft light and perfume and luxury; of sitting in such rooms, beautifully gowned, talking with people who all talked like Mr. Charlton and dressed like a Fifth Avenue window; of a tall young man who had the keys to hundreds of such rooms in his pocket. Her day of typing and filing seemed a long, dull dream, like Miss Heaton's life. "Say, Jellybean," said Bunny, lounging toward her with his conquering swagger, "whatcha say we take in a movie tonight? I got a buck, an' I don't mind givin' a treat to a poor but pure workin'-goil."

Geraldine brought her regard back to him from a vast distance. "No, thank you, Bunny," she said, shaking her head. "I can't, tonight." She had no attention to spare for Bunny and the commonplace pleasures he offered. All her thoughts were climbing, with her aspirations, to a beautiful, shining place at the top of a long, long flight of stairs.

II 

The quality of the subway had somehow changed since morning. The people, instead of being cheerful or patiently enduring, now were ugly and ill-tempered; they elbowed and jostled her viciously, and when they trod on her toes it was obvious that they did it on purpose. They smelled horrible. The air had been breathed over and over again, until there was no life in it but the swarming germs; it made her head ache dully. The clangor of the swift passage between stations beat on her nerves, the shock of the grinding stop and sudden start wrenched her bones apart. She realized for the first time that she hated the subway.

And the journey was interminable. Up to Fortysecond Street by the east-side line, fight your way through the crowd to the shuttle train, already crammed to the gunwales; wedge yourself between two fat stomachs for the roaring cross-town trip; trudge through labyrinthine passages to the west-side subway, and go through the whole weary process again. She did this twice a day, six days in the week; but just as you don't know how hot you are in your winter clothes until someone tells you it is spring, so all at once, after her imaginative glimpse of the spacious, gracious life that was John Leigh's at command, she found it insufferable.

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

At last she had walked the ultimate block and climbed the ultimate stair, and was at home. She needed no sudden revelation to tell her that she hated home; she had known it for some time. But she had never hated it as much as she did tonight. 

All the way to the flat, up through the stuffy, illlighted hallways and narrow stairs, the scent of the tenants' dinners had greeted and companioned her sausages on the ground floor, fish on the next, onions on the third- but as she reached her own landing the worst tidings rushed out and smote her in the face with a hearty buffet. Cabbage; burned, at that. When she had extricated her latchkey from her bag and opened the door, and the wheeze of the phonograph and the sight of the general litter had added themselves to the first welcome, she felt a strong desire to smash something, to throw something down the stairs, to clench her fists and stamp.

However, a child of the city who has to scramble for her bread and butter and cabbage is not long in learning that hysterics don't get you very much. Geraldine - now became Ellen again— shrugged her shoulders, closed the door, and forged on into the litter, the wheezing din and the smell that, to her, made up home, sweet home. ee 

For Pete's sake, turn off that racket!" she called, as she took off her hat and coat in the little bedroom over which Aunt Lulie's dropped garments still sprawled. "Isn't burned cabbage enough? " 

Aunt Lulie turned her fat, kind, foolish face over her shoulder to stare instead of stopping the machine. "Home, darlin'?" she squealed, above the noise. "I didn't hear ya come in. Wha' kep' ya so late?"

"Turn it off, turn it off!" shouted Ellen; and added irritably, as her tone startled her aunt into compliance and peace descended suddenly on the tortured air, "I declare, if I'd been here when that cabbage burned, you'd have stopped to tell me all about it before you put the gas out! ce 

Aunt Lulie looked hurt. "Now darlin', don't talk cross," she said plaintively, in the fat, husky voice that was her normal tone. The dinner got burnt becuz you was late; that's how it happened. Same way about the music; I was jest playin' it to cheer me up while I hadda wait fer you. My best reckid got busted, too— 'I Want My Red-hot Baby Now.' 

Seems like everythin's gone wrong this whole d-day.' this whole d-day." Her lip trembled. "Don't cry; it it's all right," said Ellen, with impatient compunction. "Hop around and get the dinner on, and I'll bring you another record on pay-day. Cheer up, Aunt Lulie; the paper says the sun's going to rise tomorrow. دو Aunt Lulie hauled herself to her feet, clutching at her clothes that seemed always on the verge of falling off her lax curves, and shuffled into the small, odorous kitchen. Ellen brushed her street garments and hung them in the closet, washed in the tiny bathroom crowded with Aunt Lulie's heterogeneous possessions, and tidied herself before the dusty mirror in the bedroom. She noticed that Aunt Lulie had been using her powder again, and her brows drew together with distaste, for Aunt Lulie's skin was oily and pimpled with red gooseflesh, and hers was white and fine and smooth.

But if she had only known it, the slattern atmosphere was a cogent force in her life, urging her continually toward that coveted higher level.

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

Dinner was dreadful; a lump of putty-colored boiled beef, a few strings of limp carrot, some sodden potatoes and the anticipated cabbage, all huddled together on one nicked platter in one despondent mass; weak tea and slabs of gummy bread for garnishing; the spasmodic roar of the elevated trains, outside the windows, for accompaniment. Ellen poked moodily at her flabby plateful. Aunt Lulie ate heartily, enlivening the repast with cheerful conversation; for she was always at her best in the midst of alimentation and sociability. "Mis' Grogan an' Mis' Alderman had another mix-up today," she recounted happily, with her mouth full. "Ya'd oughta been there. They both come out on the fire-escape with their garbage at the same time, an' they hadn't no more'n set eyes on each other but what they begun to holler. Mis' Alderman says Mis' Grogan's children banged around so much they made her dishes fall down off'n the shelves; then Mis' Grogan she says 

Mis' Alderman's old man come in full at midnight an' waked her up, standin' in the hall sayin' he was a gaslight an' singin' Brighten the Corner. Then Mis'

Grogan went on to say that when Mis' Alderman put her beddin' out on the fire-escape her flat got full o' bedbugs, Mis' Grogan's did. Then Mis' Alderman called Mis' Grogan "-Aunt Lulie raised her voice to a squall to combat the roar of a passing train — "a dirty liar. Then I stuck my head out the winda, an' I says, ' Ladies, you're fergettin' yourselves. That's a word that hadn't oughta pass a lady's lips,' I says, 'an' farthermore,' I says, 'it's true about the bedbugs, Mis' 

Alderman, 'cuz I got 'em off'n ya once myself,' I says." "I expect that went big with Mrs. Alderman," remarked Ellen listlessly. "What did she say then?"

"I shut the winda," answered Aunt Lulie tranquilly. "I never like to bandy words with neighbors; it ain't ladylike. Dearie, y'ain't eatin' your nice supper." "I'm not hungry," said Ellen, pushing back her plate. Her distaste for her surroundings seemed to have taken material form, and to be visible in the horrid mess before her - all the smells, all the chaos, all the general discontent. "I hate my life," she said suddenly.

Aunt Lulie stared at her, round-eyed; she had supposed they were sharing a highly successful social function, and the note of discord shocked her. "Why, dearie! What ya mean, hate your life? Ain't ya got a nice job an' a nice home an' plenny o' gen❜lmen fren's? 

What more d'ya want?" "I want a lot more," said Ellen, " and I'm going to have it, somehow. I'm not going to live like this all my days. There are beautiful places, and beautiful ways; why shouldn't I be in them? Some people are." And all at once the possessor and symbol of the yearned-for beauty stood vividly before her mind's eye, as recognizable and definite as the distaste made visible in the cabbage on her plate. Geraldine felt herself tingling from head to foot.

To take a journey to go up into strange, high places -to speak to John Leigh himself - What an adventure! She bent her head over her pad to hide the sparkle in her eyes; the fingers with which she gripped her pencil trembled.

As soon as the first dizziness of her excitement had passed, she set herself to make plans. When she called up the railway station, she found that the journey took nearly all day, and that she would not be able to get a train back until the next morning. She refrained from mentioning this fact to Mr. Charlton, fearing that he

would think better of so prolonged an expedition, and not let her go; but it opened wide and thrilling vistas.

Geraldine would get a cinder in her eye and never come back. She had a little money left from last week's pay-check. She was going to meet the most remarkable young man in the world, armed with the most cogent of introductions; she would find him far from the surroundings he disliked and the girls he dreaded, in a set-apart place where anything was possible. Perhaps - who knew? - she never would come back. 

By the time she went home that evening, her desk was in apple-pie order, her substitute instructed, her expense-money drawn, and the document which was her passport to John Leigh's presence safe in her pocketbook. Everything was ready for the great adventure.

The journey home was as violent, the apartment as deranged, and Aunt Lulie as chaotic as ever, but now it didn't matter. Ellen was even able to eat with a good appetite the horrific dinner of fat pork chops, greasy fried potatoes and blackened eggplant. She was leaving. all this; already it was a theory and not a condition. "Aunt Lulie," she said, pushing back her chair, "tomorrow I'm going away."

IV 

The slow train wheezed to a standstill, and Geraldine -it was as Geraldine-on-the-way-up that she had started this journey-came down its cindery steps and stood looking about her.

It was already twilight, for now in late September the days were shortening, and the sun slipped early behind the encircling summits. This was her first glimpse of real mountains. They towered grimly all around the near horizon, and made the small station and her still smaller self trivial and impertinent. Near at hand the dark foliage on their sides was splashed with vivid scarlet, but farther away the woods looked black and unfriendly, and the bare cliffs cold with menace. Somewhere, hidden from sight, the waterfall which gave the place its name splashed down over the rocks with a wet and chilly sound. In spite of the inspiration of her adventure, Geraldine shivered. She was a long, long way from the crowded companionable life which was the only life she knew, and very much alone. 

The little loose-jointed train-her only link with her accustomed world - grunted and puffed out of sight around the curve of the track, and she gave herself a shake and gathered her resolution for the next step. The nearer she came to it, the more daunting it looked. But she would never have progressed from 

Aunt Lulie's environment to Mr. Charlton's, and from

Mr. Charlton's to the outskirts of John Leigh's, if she had been the kind of person to give up before she began.

Looking about her, she found no sign of bridgebuilding activities, or indeed activities of any sort, within eye-range. The train she came by had carried no mail, and the little ramshackle station was locked and deserted. She stepped off the wide-fissured platform and started down a rough and dusty road in search of information; and at the first turn she came upon a dilapidated house, outpost of a straggling line that made up the village. Geraldine vouchsafed no reply. Her fingers closed tighter on the pocketbook that held her passport, and she lifted her chin high. She could afford to ignore the impertinences of tobacco-stained yokels; she was on her way, straight and sure, to meet John Leigh.

The way mounted steadily, companioned by the stream that flowed from the invisible waterfall, and grew rougher as it mounted; the ruts were deep and

dusty, loose stones rolled treacherously on its surface. 

By the time it joined the smooth upper road, Geraldine's breath was gone, and her pavement-trained feet were bruised and tired. She stopped by the crossroads signpost to rest a moment and look about her. 

The Honeymoon Trail - which a waggish highway commission had chosen to designate by a pair of pierced red hearts on a white-painted board-led high and perilous around the skirts of a towering mountain; the arrows on the signpost pointed right-hand to Claremont, left-hand to Lovers' Leap. It was darkening fast, and moving veils of mist hung about the summits. 

She must hurry. She took a deep breath to serve as second wind, and started on.

When she had gone some little distance along the road to the left, a smaller road, branching off it, indicated the Lovers' Leap with a sharp-pointed sign like an exclamation. It led into the deeper shadow between two hills, its goal lost in obscurity, and she followed it with quickening heartbeats. Presently she emerged on an open space where she could see lights and movement, and hear voices. The bridge must be near.

A tremor of excitement swept her. The adventure was at hand. 

Walking quickly to the scene of activity, she stopped again to peer around her. The place did not look in the least as she thought it would: she had dimly prefigured a vast, awesome structure like the Hudson bridge soaring off into the distance, and in the foreground a small marble building in the manner of a young Public Library, in which John Leigh sat regally, directing myriads of obedient minions. Instead, all she could see was a cluttered mass of steel rails and

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

lumber, a couple of large, uncouth shacks made of unpainted boards, a few dim electric lights strung on improvised poles, and a confusion of undirected movement drifting hither and thither. Where was the bridge? and where the millionaire magnificence?

Blinking and a little daunted, she paused under a tree and tried to decide what to do next. Work was evidently over for the day, and the men were scattering -some to the lighted shack, whence issued loud voices and a banging of indestructible tableware, some along the dusky road by which she had just come. Tired and hungry, talking noisily, they trudged by the place where she stood without seeing her. . . . Everybody was going somewhere. Time was passing. She must hurry. "Can you tell me," she said urgently to one of the men tramping past, "where to find Mr. Leigh? " "Huh?" grunted the man. "Over dere. By shack."

He too, intent on his own affairs, paid no attention to her; she might have been only another shadow under the trees. 

Geraldine looked hard at the place toward which he had jerked his head. In the doorway of the farthest shelter a small, insignificant building back in the edge of the wood-two men were talking, outlined by a faint streak of light from within. One was a big, burly figure in overalls; in the other she recognized the tall distinction of John Leigh. Her heart came up into her throat, and on feet winged with expectancy she crossed the rough intervening space. ee 'Mr. Leigh!" she said timidly, standing in the shadow beside him.

If Geraldine's heart had been in her throat before, now it jumped into her very mouth. He was going-out of sight, out of reach- and he didn't even know she was there! It was now or never. She took a quick step after him, breathless with nervousness. "Mr. Leigh!" she said. "Here's a paper Mr. Charlton sent you! 

"Eh?" returned John Leigh, in a preoccupied tone. "Oh yes, he telephoned me. Thanks." 

Half turning toward her in the shadow, he stretched out a hand, took the envelope from her, and thrust it into his pocket. Then he hurried off with the anxious man. Just as on those two occasions in Mr. Charlton's office, he had never seen her at all.

She stood gazing after him with a flat sense of defeat and frustration; her heart settled back into her chest again, a solid, heavy lump. Was this the end of her

golden moment? - all those miles traversed, all those high hopes harbored, for this? She had seen John Leigh, she had spoken to him, she had done her errand; and it was all and he didn't know there was such a person as herself in the world. over, Well no use standing there. He wouldn't come back again, and if he did come he wouldn't see her. For that matter, she had no longer any excuse for being present at all. Dispirited and defeated, she turned away.

Re-traversing the dark lane that led to the highway, she directed her feet that seemed shod now with lead -back along the way they had trod, so light with adventure, half an hour ago; back along the Honeymoon Trail.

But when she reached the crossways where the rough road led down to Craddocks' Falls, she stopped short.

She couldn't bear to go back to that dismal little village. 

Of all places to sit and reflect on dreams gone wrong, Craddocks' Falls must be the last in the world to choose.

Since she had to stay until tomorrow anyway, why not salvage what she could from the wreck of the adventure? The tobacco-stained man on the cottage steps had said that John Leigh stayed at the Claremont Inn. 

Why shouldn't she stay at the Claremont Inn too?

Perhaps she would meet him there; perhaps all was not yet lost.

With Geraldine, to think was to act. She squared her slender shoulders, lifted her chin resolutely, and started off along the Honeymoon Trail in the direction of Claremont.

V 

Now it was thick dusk even out on the open road; the sun had slid down the far side of the western mountains, the veils of cloud hung thick about the darkling peaks. It was almost night. The road to Claremont hugged the mountainside closely a narrow ledge, defended from the abyss only by a row of white-painted posts supporting a chain. From the gorge came the cold, the menacing sound of fast-rushing water.

Geraldine shivered. This was the first time she had ever been out at night away from the clustered lights and friendly comfortable crowds, and it was not only her body that shivered; her spirit was cold with loneliness and awe. The mountains overhung her as if they meant to fall down and crush her; their breath chilled her bones.

What little movement there had been upon the road had all gone down to Craddocks' Falls with the workmen, and she seemed to be the only person alive in all the world. . . What a terrifying, big, dark world! 

What a little lost person all alone in it! . . . Cold, so cold; and afraid she didn't know of what. . . . 

An owl hooted, with that shrill, eldritch cry which strikes even accustomed nerves like a claw clutching the back of the neck. "Oo-ooh!" cried Geraldine; and she began to run.

Those shoes, with their high heels and pointed toes;

those tired, aching feet; that road, hanging over the abyss where the icy water rushed and rumbled. . . 

And she was so tired; where was Claremont? Surely it must be near now. She was stumbling with weariness and fright. She had hardly slept at all for excitement the night before; she had been travelling since early morning, and walking for the last hour; she was tired out before the sorry anticlimax of her adventure had made her aware of it. Now she felt that even John 

Leigh wasn't worth going much farther for. Where was Claremont? 

But of course she must be almost there. 

It was so dark now that the street-lights had been turned on those sparse, forgetful lights that make the darkness of country highways visible. One of them gleamed dully a short way ahead of her, and just beneath it a white signboard held out black-printed statistics. Encouraged, she made a little tired run to it. 

Craddocks' Falls, 2 m.," said the board. Claremont, 10 m.," said the board very clearly. ee ce Geraldine stared at it with a gasp of consternation. 

To get to Claremont, she must go five times as far along this dreadful road as she had come from Craddocks'! and already she so ached with weariness and cold and fear that the distance she had traversed seemed half the circumference of the earth. 

"Oh, I can't, I can't!" she moaned. "I can't walk ten miles more in this awful darkness - not if I never see him! I wouldn't see him anyway. If I did, he wouldn't see me. I'll go back to that horrid little village and sleep in that dirty little hotel- and go home tomorrow. Good-bye, John Leigh - I'm beaten." 

Crying under her breath with fatigue and discourage-

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

ment, she began to stumble back along the road she had just come.

Now, the faint stimulus of hope removed, the way seemed even more dreadful than before. The darkness was thicker, the cold breath of the mountains heavier; out of the woods came strange, eerie night-sounds that made her hair creep on her scalp. If she had been afraid before, now she was terrified. She was too frightened to run. She walked rigidly, with that paralyzing dread of some unspeakable invisible foe just behind which no reasoning can vanquish. Even the subway, even 

Aunt Lulie's dirty odorous flat, would seem a blessed haven now.

When she had achieved about half of the return journey, she came to a place that daunted her still more. 

Some casual carelessness of nature's had loosened a boulder on the mountainside, and when it rolled downward it had carried with it a mass of earth and smaller stones that now lay heaped upon the pavement: viewed as a landslide, it was a very small affair, entirely beneath notice; but it was enough to narrow the passage considerably. On the way over, hurrying toward what she supposed to be an imminent goal, and comforted by a faint flicker of hope, she had hardly noticed it; but now one of the feeble street-lamps shone on it, and it seemed to her weary and beaten as she was to 

She stopped short before it. fill the whole road.

On one side, the mountain, which had already begun to fall and which would undoubtedly proceed to tumble down entire on the other, the dark gorge from which nothing preserved her but one slender chain, probably already beginning to disintegrate in rust and ruin! Panic clutched her. If she tried to walk

on the unobstructed part of the road, she knew she would fall into the abyss. If she tried to scramble over the rubble, she knew an avalanche would come down and crush her. Afraid to go back, unable to go forward, she stood in a paralysis of terror, numb and helpless.

She might perhaps have stood there all night; but presently, from around the turn of the curving road, came a distant hum, which rose in a rapid crescendo to a loud, purring whine; and in another minute a gleam of light shot around the curve, followed by a long, dark body. A car-coming to pass her in this perilous place, where there was not even room enough for her to pass alone! She knew she must move, must go forward or back, or else up the mountainside or down the gorge; but that deadly paralysis held her captive. 

She stood clenching her hands, holding her breath watching the car rush nearer, nearer- dully watching 

Death rush with it. However, the car stopped short, and Death stopped too; neither was moving as fast as she supposed. As a matter of fact, the powerful lights were profoundly concerned with the obstruction in the road, and the motive power behind the wheel was proceeding toward it with extreme care so slowly that the long roadster was about to pass with ease the place she had thought too narrow for her own small body; so slowly that the lights picked out her white face and the power stopped the moving wheels at one and the same instant. 

When it stopped, she still stood moveless and numb. 

She had even no quiver of emotion when the door of the car opened and John Leigh stepped out. She was, for the moment, beyond feeling; she had been so

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

frightened that it was as if she had really died, and nothing mattered any more. "What's this?" exclaimed John Leigh, in a startled voice. "What's the matter?" 

She could not answer; she moved her lips, but no sound came.

Leaving the car, he hurried past the rubbish-heap to where she stood. "Are you hurt?" he asked anxiously.

She shook her head mutely. She wasn't hurt; she wasn't anything at all. "Lost, then? You must be-wandering around this lonesome neighborhood at this time of night. Jove, you gave me a start! Where were you trying to go? 

She felt far from ready for the complicated effort of speech, but his intent eyes compelled her to it; so she swallowed convulsively, and managed a faint murmur. "To-to-somewhere-to sleep." "Somewhere to sleep!" he repeated. "Don't you know where?"

She shook her head again. The unfamiliar local names had been shocked out of her memory by terror, though overwhelming weariness kept her reminded of her errand. "Somewhere—a long way off,” she said confusedly. cc "It's amnesia, that's what it is," said Leigh, speaking aloud to himself. He gazed at her in anxious perplexity. 

Have to do something with her- but what?—I'm sorry to bother you," he added gently, "but can't you think where you were trying to go? Think hard!" 

His gentleness helped her to marshal her forces, but at the same time it broke down the barrier of numbness which had kept her misery at bay. "I was trying-

SI to go past this awful place," she answered, "and oh, I can't, I can't!" The last words broke from her in a wail, and all at once she was seized and shaken by an uncontrollable burst of weeping.

She could not know how little and forlorn she looked, set against the black night by the white glare of the car's headlights, crying like a lost child into the crook of her arm; but John Leigh knew, and being a human male he was completely overthrown by the sight. He took a long, quick step that brought him very close to her. "Don't! don't!" he besought. "Please don't cry! 

It's all right, honestly-you shan't go anywhere you don't want to. Don't cry. Poor little girl, don't cry. 

As he spoke, his arm, propelled by an instinct far older than his acquired caution, went around her slender, shaking body; and obeying an answering instinct, she moved to him and let her wet cheek rest against his shoulder. For the first time she smelled the aroma of good tweed, slightly tobacco-scented, which is the aura of well-bred masculinity; and she found it, as most women do, potently comforting. She relaxed against him, quivering, but sobbing more softly. "There, there," he soothed her, patting her shoulder. "It's all right. It's all right. Poor little kid. Don't cry. Don't try to remember. It's all right." 

Geraldine drew a long, sobbing breath. It was all right. She needn't try to remember. She needn't be afraid. This good-smelling coat was going to take care of her. "Oh-h!" she said, with a deep sigh of relief. "You're such a c-comfort!" 

John Leigh laughed huskily. "I'm glad of that," he said. "Poor child, you need a little comfort. Come

His arm around her, he piloted her to the long, low roadster, helped her up the step, and tucked her into the seat. It felt wholly unlike the leathery hardness of the taxicabs which made up her scant experience of motors; the upholstery was deep and soft, the back leaned at the angle of perfect comfort, the door shut firmly yet almost without a sound. As they purred into motion, something about the whole impression of quiet luxury touched her dulled senses, and brought them back to awareness.

Of course this was John 

Leigh's car. And she 

And she - Ellen James, alias Geraldine was driving with John Leigh! 

The thought so startled her that she sat up straight and turned to him, with words- she could not have told whether of gratitude or protest-rushing to the tip of her tongue. But he had no attention to spare for her now; he was absorbed in the ticklish task of nosing between the rubble and the boundary-chain, with only a few inches to spare on either side. His eyes were narrowed, and his mouth set in a firm line. Clearly this was not a time for conversation. 

And indeed, what had she to say? She sank back into her seat to consider. Bewildered as she was, she recognized that his kindness to her was the result of her lost and strayed condition; when she had come to him as an emissary from Mr. Charlton he hadn't even seen her; if she were to introduce herself as a traveller from New York he would withdraw into his defensive caution and drop her at the nearest crossroads.

Better let things drift for a little. She was too tired now to make any effort, anyway; she felt like a ship-

wrecked seafarer who has just been picked up by a staunch ship, and wants nothing but to lie still on the deck and be thankful that he is alive. enough. • .. 

No hurry.... · · Time They had passed the ticklish spot, but Leigh still had plenty of need to give his attention to his driving; the road, though unobstructed, was narrow and curving, and none too safe even in the day-time; the darkness pressed black and impenetrable except where the headlights bored a narrow white tunnel into it. Geraldine rested against the comfortable back with a relaxed sense of well-being. The purr of the motor drowned the frightening noises of the night, the warmth of the engine stole comfortingly about her; she watched his long, strong hands on the wheel, and blinked like a sleepy kitten.

Now they came to a place where the mountains stood apart to form a bowl, with a little town sitting compactly in the middle of it. Leigh, slowing the car under the street-lamps, turned and looked at her. "How's my passenger?" he asked. 

She smiled at him drowsily. "Very well." "Want to stop?" "No-oh no!" 

He laughed. "You're a good sport, passenger. All right then, we'll keep on a while. I want to get you to a place that's better for you than this."

They kept on through the town—which Geraldine found from the signs, without much caring, to be Claremont - and came out again into the dark night of the country. The road, still marked by the red arrows of the Honeymoon Trail, plunged now into thick woods; they, and the owls shrilling their calls

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

back and forth in a wild, uncanny antiphony, were apparently the only creatures alive; the lights bored into a gloom so thick that they seemed to cut it like an apple-corer; and she felt set apart in a land of enchantment, where nothing was real, and nothing mattered.

It came to her as a shock when, after some miles of this timeless journeying, Leigh stopped the car and leaned out to peer into the thick murk. "I thought so," he said. "Jove! I wish this had happened sooner, if it had to happen at all!"

Geraldine, aroused to attention, peered and listened too; and though she could see nothing, she was aware at once of an ominous rumbling. "Is there going to be a storm? she asked. وو "I'm afraid there is. We ought to have stopped at 

Claremont, but I thought we were leaving the clouds behind. These mountain storms are tricky. Shall we turn back? وو She thought of the little commonplace town, lighted and unglamorous, and of the magic journey into the mysterious dark, and all her desire protested against returning. "Oh no!" she exclaimed. "Let's go on!" "I said you were a good sport," he commented, smiling at her again. "We really might as well; we're almost as near Edgewood now as Claremont, and we may get there before it breaks. All right- here goes! 

He started the engine again, and they began to rush on through the dark. But, as if the pause had broken a spell, the unreal, dreamlike quality of the journey was banished. The storm was coming on apace; she could hear the crashing thunder above the humming of the car, and see the lowering, menacing mountains

by the lightning flashes. She was uneasy in thunderstorms even when they were denatured by the city; this one, naked in the wild upland night, appalled her. 

She clasped her hands tight, and quailed at every booming onslaught; and though, ashamed of her fear, she kept her lips pressed together and made no sound, she began to shiver uncontrollably. "Cold?" asked John Leigh, feeling her shoulder quiver. 

She shook her head. "N-no." 

He looked around at her with quick anxiety. "Frightened!" he said. "Don't be frightened. This won't last long; I'm going to get you into a safe, quiet place before you can say Jack Robinson."

Even as he spoke, a crack of lightning split open the sky above them, and as if it had been a leak in a reservoir, great drops of rain began to spatter down, splashing the clear windshield and the dark, smooth road. Then, suddenly, the wind, which had been roaring high up around the mountain-tops, seemed to sweep down into a funnel, coming from all directions and focussing on the spot where they were. The leak in the sky widened to let loose a deluge; they were flooded, sluiced, drenched as if with buckets; the lightning lashed tongues of white fire about them, the thunder crashed against their very cheeks.

Leigh leaned forward, peering into the welter, driving very slowly. Geraldine sat tense and rigid, her hands clenched, her breath frozen in her lungs. So they went, a little moving unit of life lashed by inhuman forces of hostility, for what seemed an interminable time—every mile a league, and the journey's end a place impossible of attainment.

Once Leigh spared a hand from the wheel to lay it reassuringly on her tight little fist. "Keep up your courage, traveller!" he said. "I'm not af-fraid," she answered valiantly, in a quivering voice. 

He gave the fist a quick pressure. "You're splendid!" he exclaimed, with a quick, warm glance.

She tried to smile, but her lips were stiff and her face set like a mask over her terror.

At last, when Geraldine was beginning to think that the end of life had come, that she had died and gone to a horrible eternity of stabbing fires and shattering crashes, she heard him give a low exclamation; and following his eyes, she saw a dim light at the side of the road, set deep among trees. While they were still alive, they were going to find a roof! Never before had she realized the blessedness of shelter. It was all she could do to keep her self-control; she wanted to cry out for joy.

Lurching, they turned off the road and nosed slowly into a narrow, rutty lane. The lights picked up a little drenched house, with two or three insignificant outbuildings huddling forlornly behind it, and Leigh brought the car to a stop in the shelter of a clump of trees. cc

The house was small, cluttered and stuffy; but it was warm and dry, and a bright fire glowed in the little stove, shining redly through an isinglass pane. To 

Geraldine it seemed like heaven. She dropped into a chair, utterly spent and thankful.

Leigh, turning back into the room after hanging his dripping outer garments on a peg behind the door, looked at her anxiously. "See here, this won't do!" he said. "You can't sit there in your wetness. Let's fix you up a bit.”

Taking her hand, he drew her to her feet, lifted off her drenched coat and hat, and hung them up beside his own. Then he fished a big clean handkerchief out of his pocket and dried her face with clumsy, gentle movements. She submitted docilely, feeling sure she looked a fright, but not caring. He hovered over her solicitously and uselessly, evidently desiring to do something, not knowing what; he smoothed her hair and gave her face a final polishing; and then, drawing the most comfortable chair the room held in front of the stove, he took her by her shoulders and set her in it. ee دو 

There! he said. "Now you're dry, and in a minute you'll be warm, and very soon after that you'll be fed. And then you'll feel lots better, little-I don't suppose you remember your name, do you?"

Leaning back in the big rocker, she shook her head drowsily. She couldn't remember if, at the moment, she were Geraldine or Ellen, and in any case it didn't matter. Safe, warm, taken care of, utterly weary, she seemed enfolded in soft thick cotton through which no troublesome thought or sensation could prick. "Poor little lost kitten!" said Leigh, looking down at her. "We're like Hänsel and Gretel taking refuge in the witch's cottage, aren't we? I'll go now and tell the old witch to scare us up some food."

She didn't know who Hänsel and Gretel were, but that didn't matter either. She smiled at him from under half-closed eyelids, and then let the lids fall altogether as he turned away. Nothing mattered, tonight. Recalled from her drowse by a sound of movement, she looked up to see him returning with a battered table, which he set down beside the little glowing stove, and toward which he proceeded to move her bodily, chair and all. "We're going to have the banquet here," he explained, "because the only other spot seems to be the kitchen, and that's chock-full with the old goody and the provender. Hungry?" 

Geraldine lifted up her nose and sniffed. "Ham!" she exclaimed ravenously. "Eggs! Oh, I'm starved!" 

He laughed delightedly. "Here goes for the firstaid, then! I've been rooting among the reserves, and

I've done pretty well. Nothing like being an engineer, used to quarrying your nourishment out of the mountainside, to make you resourceful."

As he spoke, the old woman appeared with a cloth and a loaded tray, and the dingy little room burgeoned

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

into a refectory. There was the anticipated ham and eggs, flanked by browned potatoes and peas; there was really excellent coffee, which Leigh proudly announced to be of his own making; and he had discovered some preserves, and a slab of nearly petrified fruitcake. 

Under any circumstances it would have been an endurable repast. To people hungry and tired and stormbound in mountain fastnesses, it surpassed the loftiest flights of Lucullus' premier chef.

Leigh sat beside Geraldine, and ministered to her as if she were a small spoiled child, cutting her meat and pouring her coffee and buttering her bread for her. "Shall I feed it to you?" he asked solicitously. 

This made her laugh. "No indeed! I know what to do with it well enough—just watch me!" "I love to," he answered unexpectedly. "It does me good to see the color coming into your cheeks. I'm like the old witch; I like to think how nice and fat you'll be for Thanksgiving."

She looked at him, startled; not only by the unconscious tenderness in his voice, but by a strange quiver of response which it moved in her. Rest and warmth and food were swiftly restoring her; already she was herself, not a frightened piece of lost humanity on a mountainside; and with the return of her own personality she was suddenly aware of his. From a protective agency, kind but impersonal, he was transformed into a man, young, vital, and of a wholly unexpected charm. "I think I'll call you Gretel," he added. "I have to call you something, and you've certainly been wandering in the woods without any supper. What a piece of luck it was that I found you!"

Their talk flowed with such instinctive friendship and confidence that Geraldine's only effort was not to drop her shield of anonymity; and as for Leigh, it was evident even to her inexperience that this trustful comradeship was to him like water to man thirsting in a desert.

When they had eaten all they could, and the old woman had taken away the vestiges, they fell into a warm and cosy silence. As happens inevitably with people whom nature has made sympathetic to each other, who have been through anxiety and hunger and the pleasant elemental satisfaction of feeding together, they were now old friends; they sat enfolded in fellowship as in the warmth that came through the glowing door of the little red-hot stove. Leigh smoked his pipe, with his legs outstretched and his hands in his pockets; Geraldine cuddled into the depths of the rocker, and wished she could purr. She thought no more about her plans, or even about tomorrow; she was only drowsily, perfectly content.

Presently, into the peaceful silence came the old woman who had been bustling unheeded about the outskirts for some time-with a lamp in her hand. "Your room's ready," she piped. "Right there behind ye. Good-night; hope ye sleep good." She turned away.

The two by the fire started, recalled to reality with a shock. They stared at her, aghast, as the implications of her remark came home to them. "Oh, hold on! " cried John Leigh, jumping up and hurrying after her. "Haven't you another? Another room? Another-room?"

She heard him on the second repetition, and turned back with a leering grin that showed her shrivelled gums. "Quarrelled with your bride? Well, ye'll have to make it up, 'cuz there ain't any other room. I got to sleep on a mattress in the attic as 'tis." 

Geraldine jumped up too. "Let me go with you!" she cried. "Don't want ye," returned the old woman candidly. "No space. You make it up with him. Give him a good smack. Ye'll find 'tain't hard.” 

Cackling to herself, she shuffled away.

The two were left appalled and abashed. VII WHICH PATH? 

For all her weariness, Geraldine felt as wide awake as if she could never sleep again. The thunderstorm was long since past, but the wind still wailed; somewhere near by there was a swift rushing of water, and the trees shook and rustled stormily. Moreover, within herself there was a quiver and commotion that made her feel strung with electric wires. She did not undress, except to take off her tired little shoes, for the old woman's bed was not seductive. Unfolding a patchwork quilt for cover, she lay down on the outside, and let the impressions of the day run along her tingling nerves.

It had been a wonderful day; the most wonderful she had ever known. She could hardly believe that she had seen the beginning of it in Aunt Lulie's frowsy flat. The long, long journey; the bitter disappointment; the weariness; the fright; the rescue; the storm; - and after all these, the delicious, the matchless evening, safe and sheltered, and warm with an unnamable happiness which had no parallel in her experience. She had forgotten all about John Leigh the multimillionaire, the jingler of golden keys to beautiful places; all she could think of now was John Leigh the manher friend. 

Of course he wouldn't be her friend when she told him who she was. A girl from Mr. Charlton's office, come all this distance with the deliberate purpose ...

WHICH PATH? 

67 of getting acquainted with him. When he heard that, he would turn from her in disgust, and she would never see him again. 

At this thought, a stabbing pang shot through her. She couldn't bear to have him go away and never speak to her-she couldn't! The only reason she wanted tomorrow to come was so that she could see him. . . . And tomorrow she must tell him.

There was no getting out of that; she had deceived him long enough. دو 

But the deception had been so easy! He had really done it all himself. "Amnesia," he had said; and "Can you remember your name? - and at the moment she really couldn't, she couldn't remember anything.

Afterwards he had taken it for granted that her memory was lost completely, and she had been glad to let him, because his thinking so preserved that delicious, untroubled intimacy. When he knew, it would be all over.

Well- it wasn't over yet. She still had tonight to be happy in. She banished the troubling thought of tomorrow, and concentrated on memories of the evening just past. Memories of the jolly supper by the little ruddy stove; of the merry laughter, the happy understanding; of John Leigh's eyes, so clear and blue beneath their thick brown brows, so deep and friendly when they looked at her; of his strong warm hands, and his vibrant voice-quick, sometimes, with tenderness. 

Good-night, little Gretel! " These she laid ee beneath her cheek when she tucked her hand under it on the pillow; and the rain pattered, and the trees sighed, and at last, still hugging the little warm thoughts, she drifted off to sleep.

At last, when the tumult out-of-doors had ceased and the night had sunk into exhausted calm, she too fell into a beaten, unhappy sort of peace. Right or wrong, she was not going to tell. On that decision, she slipped her hand under her pillow again, and went to sleep.

VIII  

Morning arrived brilliant and clear, new-washed by the rain, and as serene as if there had never been a tempest since the world first shrugged itself from chaos into calm. Geraldine, wakened by the sun shining full on her face, washed in the old woman's cracked china basin, smoothed her shining hair before the wavery, blue-splotched mirror, and straightened her rumpled frock. She looked pale and weary, though very pretty; her spirit felt pale and weary too; but her resolution was unaltered. She was going to take up this adventure where it had left off last night. 

Leigh was already in the little living-room, fresh and ruddy as one should be who has washed at a pump and exercised briskly in early-morning air. She noticed the clean look of his tanned skin, with the dark, clear red showing through below the cheekbones, and the bluish whites of his eyes; he looked as trained and fit as a marathon runner. She had forgotten all about his lack of movie-hero elegance now; every item of his appearance seemed exactly, unalterably right. Her heart gave a queer little jump at the sight of him. 

The morning began, as mornings nearly always do, on quite a different plane from the one where the evening left off. Neither had slept well; both were

constrained at remembering, in the full glare of day, the tenderness into which they had been betrayed by darkness and the storm; and breakfast was not ready. Hovering uncomfortably about the uncomfortable little room, they made conversation on the subjects of the weather and the view; and the old woman, squeaking back and forth on her deliberate preparations, eyed them with a lively curiosity tinged with malicious amusement.

When at last the meal was ready, they sat down on opposite sides of the table, passing the sugar with scrupulous decorum, sedulously hiding their thoughts each from the other. They were more like two slightly inimical acquaintances endeavoring to keep a precarious peace than two who had been, a few hours before, so near lovers that the falling-short is hardly worth mentioning. 

Geraldine- far from happy, yet unshaken in her resolution - watched Leigh covertly, and her intuitive imagination told her much of what was passing in his mind. He, so habituated to reserve, was ashamed and abashed at the memory of last night's impulsiveness; troubled at having, as he thought, taken advantage of her defencelessness; perplexed about what to do with her. Also he was trying, with difficulty, to fit her claim upon him into the other claims that filled his day. 

At least he accepted her as a responsibility, she thought; and she was thankful for that. She would rather be a responsibility to him than nothing at all. 

Presently, fortified by food but still with a constrained and troubled mien, he attacked her problem directly. "When I met you," he said, "you were coming from

Geraldine rose and fetched it from the bedroom, where she had used its scanty resources in making her toilet for the day. The trepidation with which she handed it to him showed her, with something of a shock, how much she had already come to treasure her false position. But although he emptied and explored the bag it had no evidence to offer beyond the noncommittal vanity aids, handkerchief and thin little purse of money, and left him none the wiser. She felt herself several degrees safer when he handed it back. "Well, that's that," said he. "Then if you've quite finished breakfast, we'd better start. I got the old goody to press your coat, and I think she just put it in your room. I'll wait for you outside." 

When she emerged into the radiant morning, Geraldine found the old woman on the doorstep, beaming with a satisfaction that told of highly successful financial dealings, and Leigh standing beside his long, beautiful car. He installed her in the snug seat and took his

own place behind the wheel, she waved a farewell to their hostess, and they swung into smooth, effortless motion.

Geraldine turned to him anxiously as they sped away. "Where are we going?" she asked. "To Claremont, first. I want to ask if anyone saw you there."

She sat very straight. A still excitement possessed her, holding her rigid. Her deception had begun to seem to her like a game, a game of deadly seriousness, the object of which was to keep him near her as long as possible. Discovery now would be sure defeat for her.

But she had nothing to fear at Claremont, where she had never set her foot in her life; and she waited in comparative ease of mind for him to prosecute his search. He went first to the railway station, and inquired of the station-master and the quorum of lotuseaters assembled for the passing of the "down train" if they had seen a young lady get off the "up train" yesterday afternoon. Someone asked what sort of looking young lady, and he glanced involuntarily at Geraldine. Thereupon the station-master and the lotus-eaters returned a unanimous negative; but she saw a meaning gleam pass furtively from eye to eye at the sight of her seated so snugly in his car, and by his heightened color she knew that he had seen it too. 

Next he went to the police-station. IX 

It was night when they reached the city. 

They had journeyed steadily all day, except for the pauses for food and fuel; up through the mountains, down to the fertile lowlands, past the blue lakes and the yellow harvest-fields, and at last, threading slowly, among the gleaming lights and crowded movement of the city. Geraldine was in a golden languor. She was very tired, but with the delicious tiredness that comes from too much fresh air and too much beauty of scenery; she was warm, lapped in the thick fleecy robe that Leigh had bought for her, and in the warmth of his presence; and, resolutely staving off all thought except that of the present moment, she was happy. 

The city seemed strange to her; it was incredible that she had left it only yesterday. But indeed - she thought dreamily, viewing it through an amber mist of enchantment — it was not the same city she had left; seen like this, from the cushions of a soft-singing car, its streets jewelled with lights and its pavements filled with pleasure-bound people in gala raiment, it was another realm than the workaday place where she daily battled for life in the subway. And she was a different person from the uncertain girl who had set forth yesterday morning, all excitement, on a precarious adventure. That girl hadn't known what she wanted. 

And that girl hadn't known John Leigh.

Her mood of dreamy unreality held while, slipping expertly through the traffic, Leigh brought the car abreast of the darkened Park and into one of the more exclusive side streets off upper Fifth Avenue. It continued while he stopped in front of a quietly elegant building and helped her out, and while she was wafted inside and up to a higher level. Presently she found herself, without being fully aware of all the steps in the transition, ensconced in a wide, low-ceiled, softly lighted room high above the city's rumble, with a door closing the two of them into warm privacy, and other doors opening on shadowy, suggested luxuries beyond. "Very tired, Gretel?" asked Leigh, looking at her anxiously. 

Not very, She smiled through her drowsiness.

John." She was conscious of nothing except content. "You're the best sport in the world. That's your bedroom, in there; you'd better go and rest, while I see about getting you something to eat. Shall I send a maid to you?" 93 "Oh, no. 

Where are we? Whose house is this?" "It's an apartment-hotel. This is your apartmentand your hotel too; yours to command. I chose it for you because it's about the quietest spot in town, and telephoned from Albany for your accommodations. 

I hope you'll like it."

She was a little disappointed that it was not his own house; but since he was in it, that didn't really matter.

"I think you'd better have something, though. I'll see what they can get for us. Go and rest while I do it, like a good child."

She entered the bedroom obediently, and as she closed the door and pressed the switch-button beside it, little rosy lights sprang into being here and there about soft ivory-tinted walls, and shed their glow on pale furniture and the roses and cream of delicate upholstery.

In a wide alcove stood a bed such as she had seen in the motion pictures, broad and low, piled with big pillows and little pillows, canopied in cream and roses. Through a doorway beyond, a bathroom gleamed silver and crystal like a china-cabinet. It was the sort of room she had dreamed of; in a rosy dream herself, she found her dream coming true.

The sight of the gleaming crystal cabinet reminded her that she did want a bath; it was the only thing needed to complete her felicity. She started the water in the wide, low tub, and came back to the chair before the rose-petticoated dressing-table to take off her clothes. The glass gave her back a flushed, heavy-eyed image that looked as unreal and blissful as she felt. She forbore to look at it closely; a half-conscious instinct told her that the only safe course for her now was to keep herself as unreal and unsentient as that reflection. 

Presently, into her trance drifted Leigh's voice, in one of those broken monologues associated only with the telephone. He spoke low and guardedly, and at first she hardly heard or heeded him; but presently a more insistent note caught her ear and pricked her to attention. 

"No, I want the doctor himself, please; it's urgent.

Granville, right away - whether you can or not. I've got a girl here who's lost part of her mind.—No, no, it's no joke; it's deadly serious. • · • A doctor and a nurse! That would be the end of everything. They would know her, at a glance, for an impostor; they would tell John; she would be banished into the outer darkness. She would never see him again. 

Oh but she couldn't bear that! If John were to go out of her life now, life would be over for her. She could not, must not lose him. John. John.

She began to think hard, her heart beating a tattoo of panic. Perhaps she could go through with it. It had been pitifully easy, without any intention at all, to deceive John; now that she was on her guard, she might be able to deceive these others too. Amnesia seemed to consist only in a blank past and a blank stare, and this doctor had apparently said he didn't know anything about it anyway; no one knew she had disappeared except Aunt Lulie; and even Aunt Lulie didn't know where she was. Her lips shut tightly on a resolution to try.

By the time she had bathed and dressed again, her

courage had revived, and she felt a sort of desperate readiness for the undertaking. She studied her reflection critically. Fear had whitened her cheeks and darkened her eyes, making her look strained and unnatural. Her frock was wrinkled with two days' wearing, the sheer white collar creased and rumpled; and while this distressed her fastidiousness, it added to the effect of wan forlornity. Staring into the glass, she made her eyes wide like those of a lost girl she had read about, and found that she did indeed look startlingly strange and pitiful. She opened her door and went out. 

Leigh was waiting for her, with his cheerful friendliness thinly veiling anxiety; and in a minute a waiter, in mulberry-colored livery, appeared with a tray of silver-covered dishes. There was rich hot chicken soup, and delicate fingers of toast, and buttery oysters fragrantly simmering in their own juice; their deliciously mingled aroma floated temptingly above them, but she shook her head to its invitation and Leigh's coaxing, too tense and uneasy to eat. He would not sit down at the little table unless she did; and he was still coaxing, and she still refusing, when there came another knock at the door, and the doctor entered.

XI 

When she arose in the morning, however, she arose as Gretel Vail. She had no intention of turning back; she had cast off Geraldine in the night as completely and finally as Geraldine had cast off the lowly Ellen. 

With her adaptability, she began at once to think of herself as Gretel, to set a new standard for and form a new conception of herself. In this undertaking the strangeness of her surroundings was a great stimulus; no one named Ellen or Geraldine had, in her experience, slept between cool linen sheets, bathed in a wide alabaster-like tub, or looked from her bedroom window at the Park instead of the "L." Her fastidious senses eagerly drank in the beauty, the quiet, the lovely cleanliness. But it was not love of luxury now that strengthened her will and confirmed her resolution; it was love of John.

She must keep John in her life. All scruples and uncertainties, whatever their nature, must be sacrificed to that imperative need.

The first lion in the path was Miss Jenks. She had a strong suspicion that Miss Jenks distrusted her; certainly there was no love lost between them. They ate breakfast in a state of armed neutrality, passing each other the marmalade, watching each other warily. 

Gretel discovered - what she had been too preoccupied to observe the night before-that Miss Jenks sniffed;

worse, she sniffed in a definite rhythm, on an average of three times to a minute. It wasn't a practical sniff; she had no cold; and that made it all the more exasperating. Gretel found herself listening for it, counting off the intervals, her annoyance increasing at each recurrence. On her part, Miss Jenks watched her new charge narrowly, like a critical step-mother on the lookout for solecisms, and, sniffing, refused to allow her a second cup of coffee. It was not a convivial repast: by the end of it their mutual dislike had set and crystallized.

When the meal was over, they talked about Gretel's needs. Miss Jenks made a competent list, noting what size and how many of each. Then she put on her hard black hat and her shiny black coat, and departed, sniffing through her unpowdered nose. "I shan't be gone long," she said.

No need to tell Gretel that. She knew it already. 

She had been counting on this brief release, planning ever since she woke how to get the greatest benefit from it. First, she must make a friend, and if possible an ally, out of the substitute dragon. Second, she must contrive somehow to put herself in the way of acquiring information about her "case" before encountering the specialist. It would take quite a bit of doing, and she was impatient to begin.

The deputy dragon proved to be the maid whose services went with the apartment. She was a trim, rather pretty girl, with an immaculate uniform and the smooth, deferential manner of the well-trained underling; but Gretel observed her bright peeping eyes, and smiled to herself. She knew this girl; she had ridden in the subway with her hundreds of times, had brushed

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

elbows with her in dozens of cheap restaurants. This was another child of the city, on her way from maid to hat-check girl, to manicure, to-who knows?-perhaps the night-club or the chorus. She understood such girls, and could deal with them. "What's your name?" she asked.

The maid looked up from her dusting with a film of impersonal deference drawn over the inquisitiveness of her glance. "Marie, madam."

She conducted her charge to the ground-floor-whispering an explanation to the watchful-eyed clerk in the lobby -and made breathless haste to introduce her into a large, luxurious room, lined with books in rich crimson and russet bindings.

This was better than Gretel had hoped. She knew that the book-shops hereabouts were so far and few that she would have been lucky to find one at all in her brief time of freedom; and having no money, she would have had to borrow from Marie, which might have involved her in difficulties. But here, as soon as she had time to cast an exploring glance about the walls, she discovered the tall, multitudinous black backs of an encyclopedia in the corner, and knew that fortune was on her side.

Hastening to the corner, she pulled out the first volume and plunged into it. The article on Amnesia was short; she read it through three times, and acquired the information that nobody knew very much about it, and the names of two easy-sounding books on mental
