Miss Jenks, moving quickly and efficiently, got a thermometer and put it into her patient's mouth, and began making rapid jottings on a little memorandum pad. Then-sniffing-she sent the round-eyed Marie to the telephone in the hall, with murmured instructions to get Dr. Frederick Hazelton's office at once.

Gretel, sucking the thermometer placidly, permitted herself a small satisfied twinkle. This round seemed to be hers.

Now the contest was fairly on, and with a large and varied field of adversaries. They came and went doctors, inquiry agents, people from the Bureau of Missing Persons, all the complicated machinery for finding the wealthy lost; and Gretel engaged them all singlehanded.

The doctors were the hardest; especially that keeneyed, quiet-voiced, subtly probing Dr. Hazelton. She was in terror of him. But perhaps her terror helped her cause, for, beside giving her a genuine nervous tension that widened her eyes and quickened her breath, it made her wits so sharp that not an item of her newfound lore escaped her. Shopping for hats and frocks with Miss Jenks, she had succeeded in securing a little

IIO HONEYMOON TRAIL 

money and time to herself in a book department, and had bought and concealed between novels one of the informing volumes named in the encyclopedia. Poring over it in bed, she had learned many valuable things as that amnesia sufferers may have sudden erratic returns of memory, that sounds and scents may revive associations, that there may be complete amnesia on some subjects and partial on others, that loss of memory is sometimes accompanied by melancholy and sometimes by irresponsible gayety. At first, with a natural desire to show off her wisdom, she displayed several symptoms at once, and Dr. Hazelton looked at her sharply, and brought another keen-eyed man to help examine her. 

After that, grown wiser, she confined herself rigidly to the phenomena ascribed to the case of one Miss A; and then she could see that she was making a real impression, and took a certain pride in it. 

Miss Jenks was the next hardest. She was always there; her sharp eyes, glinting behind the glint of her glasses, were on every movement; she watched every word and look-always sniffing, it seemed, for lapses. 

At first Gretel sometimes felt that, even to win John, she couldn't bear it. But in a few days it developed that there were alleviations. When the nurse found how tractable and well-disposed her patient was, she not only took the patient on the prescribed walks and drives, but went out herself, an hour at a time, for what she described as "a breath "; and then Marie stayed with the invalid, and they played rummy. And presently, in her invaluable book, Gretel discovered the case of Miss X, who was subject on an instant's notice to sudden lapses into deep sleep. After that it was child's play.

III 

The Missing Persons inquirers were rather fun. She found in the newspaper a roster of the people for whom they were hunting, and adopted bits from one individual and another as her fancy suggested. The agent who usually came to investigate her was a thick, short, earnest little man, who peered at her hard with slightly bulging eyes as if she were a new kind of beetle, and breathed snuffily through his nose like a dog taking up a scent. Simulating the traits of 

Dorothy M. from St. Louis, she had him fairly panting.

When he was on the point of telegraphing Dorothy's relatives to come and complete the indentification, she switched to Mabel L. of Baltimore; and each time a young and pretty girl appeared on the list she annexed a new set of attributes. The agent had never had so interesting a search; his round, earnest face was red with perpetual excitement; and Gretel's enterprise acquired a spark which helped her to keep from dwelling on its less ingratiating aspects.

The prize of battle and the motive power and the joy of life, of course, was John. He came two or three times a week, making the long journey eagerly, staying with her to the last moment permitted him by Miss Jenks; and each time he came it was more of an understood thing that he must come, that his coming was predestined and inevitable. Now, in his presence, she had no time at all for scruples; she could think only, entirely of him, of how dear he was, of how he must, must, must belong to her. Her words, her voice, her manner, every detail of her new personality was regulated by the desire of pleasing him; Gretel Vail was in fact, as in name, almost wholly his creation. 

And even in the enchantment through which she saw

IIHONEYMOON TRAIL 

him, she could perceive that he was changing too. At first he was still subject to lapses into shyness and constraint; sometimes, especially in the presence of Miss 

Jenks, he began an interview as stiffly as if they were in a Park Avenue drawing-room. Also, her aloneness and lack of natural protectors set her apart, for a time, in a sort of sanctity, which made him keep a careful guard on his natural impulses. But as the days passed, and no one appeared to claim her, and it became more and more evident that everyone held him responsible for her, the restraining bonds of caution and of chivalry wore very thin, and only the constant presence of one or another of the official observers held the swelling emotion in check at all.

-- XII 

The relation between the two had gone on in this way- vague yet increasingly recognized, recognized almost to the point of avowal, then back to vagueness again for nearly a month; and now the situation was so delicately poised that a word, a breath, would topple it into irretrievable definiteness. Everyone knew that 

John loved Gretel; the shrewd-sniffing Miss Jenks knew it; the red-faced investigator knew it; even the detached, scientific Dr. Hazelton knew it and he knew too, the best of all, what the presence of her protector did to Gretel's heartbeats. But yet the word had not been said, the breath had not been breathed; they were still two strangers, separate and alone, instead of the palpitating, perfected one they both longed to be. 

On a day toward the end of October, a golden day of the year's late perfection, John appeared at the apartment early in the afternoon. Gretel, having been driven by Miss Jenks' omnipresence into one of those deep sleeps demonstrated by Miss X, was in her room, with the door closed; but she would have known his voice and his step through ten closed doors, and she started up, instantly alert. "Hello, Miss Jenks! Where's the little patient? he asked, with the uninhibited eagerness she had released in him.

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

"Good afternoon, Mr. Leigh. She can't be disturbed; she's in one of those comas she's so subject to." Miss 

Jenks spoke severely, signifying disapproval of the intrusion, of the coma, and of the situation in general. 

Gretel jumped up and ran to the door in her negligée, afraid that John would get away before she could stop him. ce "I'm awake now!" she cried, flinging the door wide. John!" "Gretel!" cried John. He was still on the threshold of the little foyer, hugging a huge box; but at sight of her he dropped the box and strode across the room with shining eyes.

Gretel, in these days, was really a sight to make most eyes brighten. A month of delicate living and intense aspiration had altered her visibly. She had lost the gloss of pert sophistication that had been an asset when she had to battle for her life in the subway, and in its stead had come a wistful, seeking look which was infinitely more appealing: Gretel wondering at new and beautiful things whose existence had been unknown to Geraldine. That part of the change was involuntary, and wholly unconscious. And with it went an external alteration which was deliberate and intentional - the metamorphosis of a pretty Saturday's-child into a beautifully turned-out young woman of the world. For the first time in her life she had money to spend, and she had spent it with enjoyment; but her native good taste had not deserted her, and instead of bedizening herself like a shop window, as most of her former mates would have done in her place, she had changed from a clever imitation into an article of genuine beauty. Her hair was a dark, shining nimbus around her wistful little face, her

IIS skin fine as a flower-petal; she was wearing an embroidered Chinese coat of lustrous black satin, the lining and collar of which were a soft orange-pink that lighted her brunette pallor exquisitely; and the silken garments showing beneath it, the delicate underwear and sheer shining stockings, were all of the finest perfection. Geraldine had ceased, externally at least, to be a Second, and had become, in Gretel, definitely a First. "O Gretel, you're lovely!" said John, in an uncontrollable burst of rapture, as he took her hands. get lovelier every minute." ee ee You

It took her a matter of five minutes to dress in one of the slim, exquisite frocks with which her wardrobe was now well supplied, a smart little hat to match, and the sumptuous coat; but quick as she was, John was pacing impatiently up and down, watching her door, when she emerged. He caught her arm in a caressing grasp, and sped her on to the outer door; and in a minute they were in the street, oblivious of Miss Jenks' illconcealed disapproval and the admiring gaze of the lift-boy, and indeed of everything but each other. 

The air was tingling with the gayety that seems the special property of a crisp autumn day in the city. 

Along the Avenue the long shining motors slid

smoothly, bearing people who looked, for once, blithe and brilliant in their elegance; the sidewalks were full of shifting, colorful movement; in the Park the golden 

October foliage hung in the trees like sunshine, and the children laughed and ran. Gretel's mood sparkled into a matching joyousness; she laughed as the children and the sunshine did; and Leigh looked at her, whenever he could take his eyes from his driving, with unconcealed adoration. "Having a good time, Gretel?" he asked her. "Oh, such a good time! Such fun to be alive!" she answered, glowing. "You're such a clever John, to invent such a marvellous day!" "It got that way since you came into it," said Leigh, gloating over her. "You're the clever inventor; you've made the whole world over."

Slipping through the traffic, they crossed the Park, and came to where the river stretched its shining length like a sleek, lazy animal in the sun. Battleships lay at anchor upon it, tugs and pleasure craft wove up and down it; it gleamed like something precious in the warm autumn light. To roll along so smoothly beside it, making as little effort as the water itself, while the city slid behind, and the gray face of the Palisades grew ruddy with the tracery of crimson vines .. and the satinsoft fur folded her so luxuriously . . . and John sat so close beside her, tense with awareness of her, strong, tender, and dearer than she had ever supposed anybody could possibly be. It was almost more than Gretel could bear; and suddenly her gayety melted in a delicious aching ecstasy that was half joy and half pain. 

She clasped her hands together tight; it was so perfect, it couldn't last.

XIII 

Nowadays Gretel vibrated between extremes of happiness and misery such as her day-by-day, superficial life had never known. John's love, adoring, enfolding, immeasurably deep, was a revelation of bliss she had never dreamed of; her own love, that asked for nothing but the privilege to give, was another revelation. 

When she dwelt on these ineffable dawn-flushed peaks, she was in heaven. But always there was the muddy earth of her deception, which could never be forgotten for more than a minute, below; and below that, again, was the bottomless pit of despair-John, love, everything lost — into which she must fall if she ever told the truth. There was little danger of her straying too long in Elysium while she walked over such a quagmire. 

The wedding was to be at once. That was one advantage of her situation; no family to consult, no lists to make out, no invitations, no fuss and no mess. John had to spend a few busy days at his bridge, preparing to leave it to its own devices while he took her away; she had to add a few lovely garments to her sumptuous store; and that was all. 

One thing, however, she must do before she set out upon her new life as Mrs. John Leigh; she must get in touch with Aunt Lulie. Poor Aunt Lulie had sat, a fat and pressing weight, on her chest during all this month, but the difficulties of communication had been too

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

rigorous to overcome; it was impossible to telephone, even when Miss Jenks was out, for her calls had to be made through the office, and everyone in the hotel knew that she had lost her memory and all her previous acquaintanceship; she was never allowed to leave the house alone; and of course, having no friends, she could have no visitors. But at last, after some brain-cudgelling, she hit upon a scheme artful enough to circumvent all the official watchdogs. She complained to the doctor of nervous pains in her arms and legs, and prevailed on him, by a little artful suggestion, to prescribe massage; that achieved, she posted—unobserved, in the letterchute in the corridor a note to Aunt Lulie, with careful instructions to present herself as the masseuse the next day, during the time which the nurse now kept inviolate for her outing; and then she told Marie - who, within the narrow limits permitted by discretion, was her staunch confederate-that the nurse was jealous of the doctor and would not like the masseuse he sent, and that it would be better to keep her in a good temper and let her suppose this Mrs. Graves to be someone in the employ of the hotel. By these Macchiavellian maneuvers she contrived that the interview should be conducted in complete privacy, with no one questioning anyone else about the alien presence. 

It worked out perfectly. John, who was in the intoxicated stage of love where everything is seen through a rainbow mist and heard through soft music, assented unhesitatingly; he didn't care especially for Miss Jenks himself, he said, and any masseuse reccommended by Dr. Hazelton must be all right; and all he cared about -said John, crushing all her fingers into the scope of one comprehensive kiss-was to get his Gretel to himself and let everybody in the world go hang. So as soon as Miss Jenks appeared he informed her, in the courteous but authoritative tone he used to the people he employed, that as he was about to take Miss Vail away she would cease to need professional attendance, and he would take care that Miss Jenks suffered no inconvenience in consequence.

Knock-out for the nurse! thought Gretel triumphantly. 

Miss Jenks, scenting a substantial compensation check, made no protest; but her piercing glance stabbed her patient. "Very well, Mr. Leigh," she said. "I'll leave, since you wish it. But I'll come back some day before long, to see what progress Miss Vail me, Mrs. Leigh-is making. I'm too interested in her case to lose sight of it."

After all, the fight was not yet over.

XIV  

And so they were married.

Gretel all in white, shimmering and misty; white of moonsheened gown, white of filmy stockings and gleaming shoes, white of virginal veil enveloping her from head to foot; as white as a stainless spirit. She had thought, this being so small and silent a wedding, that she would put on one of her trig little hats and the beautiful fur coat and slip out with her lover to the City Hall, or wherever weddings were achieved with the minimum of pomp and circumstance. But John, beneath his artificial reserve, had a deep vein of sentiment, preserved in boyish freshness by his lonely life. It appeared that he had always cherished a dream of leading a shining white bride to an altar of white roses; he couldn't have her married in anything but a weddinggown and veil. "I want you to look as white and innocent as you are, sweetheart," he said. 

As white and innocent as she was. rate she had the white gown. • · 

Well, at any

There was no one there at all, except the clergyman, and Marie and the maroon-liveried Fritz for witnesses. 

Miss Jenks was already gone; Gretel dared not let Aunt 

Lulie who could never refrain from weeping even at alien and indifferent weddings-appear, for fear of an emotional outburst; and John sedulously avoided ad-

mitting his world to her, partly to protect her from nervous strain, partly because his own sensitiveness to inquisitive eyes was intensified in her regard. He invested her with something of the Melisande quality, the strangeness and the pathos of a "petite princesse de lointain "; he wanted to hold her close to him and shield her from every breath. "I, Gretel, take thee, John. 

Even the name was false. Married to John- to John of all people, the soul of straightforwardness and truth under a lying name. 

But she dared not begin remembering; so precariously poised was her house of life that shifting one brick might pull the whole structure down. . . . For that matter, even the name she would have remembered was not her own. Lies, all lies. She would rather lie all her life than give up John! 

Her eyes flashed, and the clergyman—a bald, uninquisitive curate from the nearest church-gave her a glance of mild surprise across the white-rose altar. "I, John, take thee, Gretel. . . ." His voice quivered with love and earnestness, and he looked at her with all his heart in his deep eyes. No lies there. ... 

They were man and wife. John kissed her on the lips, with immeasurable tenderness and gentleness. The clergyman shook her hand, and called her, with a simper of conscious jocularity, Mrs. Leigh. Marie helped her dress in a smart, mint-new beige frock and a little hat to match and the fur coat, and they were ready to go. 

Marie's eyes were full of tears. In a forlorn attempt to add what she evidently felt to be a requisite touch of gayety, she flung confetti after them, from the apartment door, with a nervous and futile gesture. It spilled

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

on the maroon velvet carpet, and drifted back against her in a variegated cloud. None of it reached the bridal pair.

Gretel on her wedding journey.

John had bought a beautiful new car for one of her many wedding gifts, all shining glass and soft upholstery and warmth and luxury; and in it she rode as not 

Cleopatra, not Semiramis nor Queen Elizabeth nor

Marie Antoinette ever rode-enclosed from every adverse wind, cushioned from every jar, skimming the long miles like a swallow. John always beside her driving easily and negligently with one arm, holding her close with the other; stopping the car in a secluded spot, every few miles, for another kiss. . . .

They drove north, in the hazy late-autumn sunshine, toward the uplands. Everything was golden; golden stubbly fields, golden light that laid a glamour on common things, golden enchantment of happiness in the heart; and it was the second day before she even thought to inquire where they were going. She moved in such a magic world of new joys that practical details seemed not to matter at all. But in the afternoon the road, winding between mountains, began to offer a dim suggestion of familiarity; and presently, spying a small white signboard with two transfixed red hearts on it, she looked quickly into her husband's face. Gretel pulled herself together with a great effort. If 

John were to have regrets and scruples too, it would be fatal; there would be no more happiness for either of them. She must be man enough to pay the price alone, and to pay it in secret. She forced her eyes to meet his steadily. "John, tell me—do I act as if I regretted it? Have you ever seen anybody as much in love—as absolutely besotted as I am? If you want to know, I'll tell you something; that very night, when you thought I was asleep, I was lying awake too-thinking how sweet it would be to travel the Honeymoon Trail- with you!" 

His arms engulfed her, his mouth was on hers. "Oh, you darling-you precious, precious sweetheart!" he murmured, between kisses. "No man ever had as much happiness as you've given me. Sweet, sweet little bride, we'll travel the Honeymoon Trail together-forever! "

Part of her remorse was eased away by the telling of this much truth; and his love and her determination banished the rest. The Honeymoon Trail was an enchanted road in the sunshine; lapped in the golden gorgeousness of autumn foliage, it ran smooth and serene as far as the eye could reach. The mountains, which at first sight had so intimidated her, now seemed to hover caressingly. Love and security encompassed her. . . . Comfortable words that she had heard long ago in Sunday-school flowed through and about her

thoughts, shaping themselves to suit her mood. 

Surely devotion and happiness shall follow me all the days of my life, and I will dwell on the Honeymoon 

Trail ... forever. 

They followed the Trail, first in one direction and then in the other, until it dipped into the lowlands, then they went back to the uplands again. Travelling by northern roads among the woods and lakes, for the first time Gretel learned to know and love the rough, kind, dirty face of Nature. John bought her clothes such as she had never worn stout boots, knickers, a warm woolly sweater and cap — and they went tramping and climbing. Her muscles hardened, and her flower-fine skin grew rosy with health. They learned the exaltation of standing silent, side by side, on mountain-tops, and the fun of picnicking, with laughter, in the sheltered hollows. It was a glorious time.

But when the three weeks they had allotted for their journeying were over, although they were more in love than ever, they were ready to go home. Leigh was the sort of Lovelace who could not love his Gretel so much, loved he not his bridge almost as well; and in her, energy was a fountain and industry a habit. They had had enough holidaying for the moment, and were willing to settle down.

It was not until they turned their faces southward that they began to discuss their future arrangements. 

John had taken it for granted that they were going back to the Granville, where he had kept the apartment; but as soon as she heard this project broached, Mrs. 

John vetoed it emphatically. The place was associated in her mind with the hateful beginnings of her decep-

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

tion, with Miss Jenks' peering and sniffing, with her own fears of doctors and of discovery; she disliked it, and wanted to forget it. Now was the time to make a fresh start. Moreover, she wished to familiarize herself with John's background, his ways of living and of thinking; she was wise enough to know that the appeal of her strangeness and pathos-at first such a potent attraction - would lessen as its newness wore away, and she wanted to measure up to his standards when he should see her with a clearer eye. For these reasons she wanted to begin her new life in John's own home. 

John, however, showed no enthusiasm for this plan. 

He loved the enclosed, the cloistered quality of their love; he wanted to keep his bride all to himself. His whole association with the big house on Park Avenue was one of movement and lavish entertainment and the semi-public ceremonial of the fashionable wealthy, and the thought of putting his Princesse de Lointain in the place of his hard, worldly mother offended him. He did not in the very least want to take her to his home. 

They found a compromise, however, highly satisfactory to both; John's bachelor apartment. He had kept it for several years as a refuge from the cold brilliance of his mother's court, and though he went there very little now, it was always in readiness. He had only to jump out of the car and pause a minute at the nearest telephone, and the Japanese home-guard would have dinner waiting for them at whatever minute they chose to eat it. To Gretel, accustomed to machinery that wheezed and creaked in its motion, this smooth celerity of wealth was a perpetual marvel. 

So, well after dusk of the short November day, the beautiful new car drew up before an unpretentious

building in a down-town side-street. Mr. and Mrs. 

John Leigh were greeted by a dignified but beaming old 

Negro doorman, in a subdued navy-blue uniform which faintly suggested a retired admiral, were bowed into a narrow foyer, and conveyed upward in a small, intimate elevator. When they emerged at the fifth floor, they found the apartment door open and the Japanese on the threshold, showing all his teeth in a crinkled Oriental smile. In another minute the door had closed behind them, and Mr. and Mrs. John Leigh were at home. 

Gretel looked about her with eager curiosity, for this was the first time she had seen John in his own habitat. 

The building was an old one, for New York; the rooms were high-ceiled and spacious, hung with dark, rich stuffs, and filled with heavy leather furniture and a masculine clutter of guns, golf-clubs and fishing-rods. 

To her amazement, the furniture was distinctly shabby; she had supposed that everything pertaining to the daily life of millionaires was beautiful and elegant, but John had nothing that had not seen hard service, and there was one chair deeply hollowed, sagging at the springs and roughened at the arms which would not have been out of place in Aunt Lulie's establishment. The walls were lined with books, and on the hearths wood fires snapped and crackled. The whole effect was masculine, sombre and comfortable; and a distinct shock to the bride.

John, too, looked about him appraisingly, seeing the place for the first time. He observed the shabbiness and clutter, and Gretel's surprised silence, and his face fell. ce 

It won't do, won't do at all," he said ruefully. "I didn't realize how bad it was. I've liked the old dump,

Gretel caught the look and rose to it. "We'll do nothing of the sort!" she answered quickly. "I think it's lovely here. I don't want it changed a bit. I love your home, Johnny; I want to stay in it." The smiling Japanese informed them, from the doorway, that dinner was "rady," and after a hasty touching-up in John's barren bedroom they went to the table. 

With the amazing efficiency of his race, the seneschal had, in the two hours since the telephone announced their coming, purveyed them a banquet; hors d'oeuvres in piquant and intriguing forms, rich clear soup, chicken roasted to a crisp perfection, succulent salad, an intricate, esoteric sweet of whipped cream and chestnuts.

Gretel welcomed it with surprise and delight, John with hearty hunger. They took it with ceremony and a bottle of wine; their first meal in their own home. 

While they ate, they talked about their plans; and the painful fact emerged that John must go to the bridge early the next morning. This dismayed Gretel. She had not expected the honeymoon to end so abruptly. ee

anybody but Gretel and John Happy-Ever-After." He picked her up bodily, carried her to the study, and ensconced the two of them in the worn leather chair before the hearth.

The room was still and peaceful. The firelight danced lightly on the brass andirons and fender, and picked out the flecks of gold in the bindings of the books; the noise of the city came in a muted grumble through the curtained windows. The two sat lover-close in the big shabby chair. Mr. and Mrs. John Leigh at home.

John rested his cheek on his wife's soft hair, in silent, rapturous content. Gretel sat still and rigid, her heart thumping at the narrowness of her escape, her mind quivering at the thought of the dangers that beset her in the midst of all the warmth and quietness and safety. 

Was that woman so powerful that, banished forever, she must keep jumping out like a jack-in-the-box?

Who would have thought the dried-up creature had so much blood in her?

Yet, if she had been wise enough to know it, the danger which threatened her lay not in the banished Miss Jenks, but in the troubled spirit working restlessly within Ellen James, alias Geraldine, alias Gretel Vail, alias Mrs. John Leigh. It's hard to evade an enemy within your own gates.

XV 

Aunt Lulie was in paradise. To live in warmth and luxury, amid the purring of wood fires and the silent ministrations of a proficient Oriental, to eat delicious foods at frequent intervals, and to draw a salary for doing so, was a height of felicity which her most sanguine dreams had never reached. The fact that she pursued this easeful course in the company of her valued niece, and that her benefactor had given her an untrammeled hand in the matter of expenses, gilded the lily to an almost unbearable effulgence. "I will say, darlin'," she remarked, when — John having departed and an empty peacefulness descended heavily on the establishment - she sat before the fire and gloated, "I seen gen❜lmen before, but I never seen one in the class with yours. 'If ya see anythin' ya don't want, ast fer it,' is his motter. I knowed he'd be classy, but I'm here to tell the world he's swell— an' I don' mean maybe." "What did he say to you, Aunt Lulie?" asked Gretel, listlessly. This first parting from her busband had depressed her profoundly, and except for a feeble flicker of satisfaction in her aunt's ecstasy, she found her world all dark.

Gretel's spirits did indeed rise rapidly as she adjusted

herself to the new conditions. The strain was over, the dishonesty put behind her; she and John were established on a permanent basis, she was living in his home, she was part of his life; and when the bridge was finished they would always be together. She stifled all the lingering uneasiness, and resolutely set herself to enjoy life.

At once she found another world opening out before her. Shopping for Aunt Lulie's boudoir, her eyes feeding on a myriad lovely hues and fabrics, she suddenly realized that now her beauty-hunger could be fed; and for the first meal she tackled the sombre bedroom she shared with John. She bought not only bright, soft colors for hangings, but a wicker chair or two, and a sewingmachine; and forthwith burst into a happy flare of domesticity, stitching curtains and covers, rearranging and renovating. The result was so successful, and 

John, when he returned from the mountains for his semi-weekly visit, was so delighted with it, that she carried her campaign of improvement further. For the next step, she concentrated the most damaged and untidy of his possessions in a completely masculine den, and feminized the spacious living-room into a gracious combination of soft color and comfort. John was even more delighted with this achievement. Like most men, he had no objection to beauty provided he could stretch his legs in it, and like most bridegrooms he regarded any evidence of capacity in his bride as a miracle of genius.

He urged her on, and she, full of enthusiasm, banished the makeshift dining-room furniture, which had neither aesthetic nor sentimental value, and spent several soulsatisfying days matching autumn-hued rugs and hangings to Circassian walnut.

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

The process of home-decorating opened another set of the Argus eyes that she was developing all over her eager mind. While Aunt Lulie hunted happily for golden oak and broke out in a violent rash of poppyspattered cretonne, she studied, with fascinated absorption, subtle harmonies of line and color that she had never been aware of before; and by the time she had finished her shopping, she had acquired a new set of valnot only in regard to the price of Toile de Jouy. 'It's lovely," she commented to Aunt Lulie, gazing at the Chinese blue and old rose which now warmed and soothed the living-room, "but I'd like to do it again. 

In terra-cotta and ivory; or in saddle-color with splashes of tangerine, perhaps. There are so many lovely tints

It ain't aw-fat to use the flag fer anythin' less'n funerals 'n' balls, dearie; I thought ya knowed that." "I'm living and learning," said Gretel, thoughtfully. When the abode was finished and regular daily life set in, the new sets of eyes went on opening. John was

gone five days of the seven, Aunt Lulie was shopping for the gold slippers she had longed for all her life, or at the movies, or snoozing contentedly among her poppies; and Gretel-savoring real leisure for the first time began on the books. At first she viewed them with a purely aesthetic eye, noting the rich charm which their varicolored bindings, picked out with gold, lent to the color scheme, rearranging them in more harmonious ranks. Then, as she handled them, she began to look inside the covers; carelessly at first, soon with growing absorption.

She had always read, in her alert city-child way, whatever came to her hand-the daily and Sunday papers, the magazines with the brightest covers. During the month when her chief objective was escape from Miss Jenks, she had read a good many volumes of the well-bound fiction in the hotel library, and a few new novels that she picked up in the shops for the sake of their attractive jackets. But now, for the first time, she was turned loose among books-books bought by the volume instead of by the yard, lived with and loved, showing pencil-marks, and opening of their own accord. 

She began by reading the places John had marked. 

Sometimes they revealed a new point of view, or made clear an established one which had puzzled her in him; sometimes things he had quoted came back to her with new force, or she recognized the source of some idea he had so wholly adopted as to forget its author. Then for the first time she perceived for she had never before had time to indulge an observant person's natural bent toward analysis-how varied a patchwork is personality, and how great a part in the piecing of it is played by books. 

---

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

With that she began to read in earnest, resolved to build up a mind that could walk boldly alongside of John's. She read without system or coördination, but with keen pleasure, her thirsty mind lapping up the many-flavored stream greedily. She had learned from one of her books on mental abnormalties of a man who, knocked unconscious by a fall from a horse, had awakened with the completely blank mind of an infant, and had filled the blank with matter entirely unknown to him in his previous life, so that presently he became a new person. She was almost like that, she thought; beginning all over again. 

Then suddenly the inspiration struck her—why not be a new person? She knew that there had been amnesia subjects who had never recovered. If she elected to be one of them, she had everything to gain and nothing to lose. Then there would never be any danger of 

John's discovering her deception. She could begin afresh, Mrs. John Leigh whom nobody had ever known, sans peur et sans reproche. . . . The thought fired her with enthusiasm; and eagerly, ardently, she began to carry it out.

Nothing could come out of the past, she assured herself, if neither she nor he would let it. She was safe now. 

And the happy days flew by.

XVI  

Now that even the possibility of a past was buried forever, the present took on renewed vitality. Of course both Gretel and John were living mainly in a radiant future, when the bridge should be finished and they should be always together; but meantime life was good, and they both took hold of it with increased assurance. 

Inevitably, Gretel began reaching out new tentacles to the world about her. Her hungry beauty-sense richly fed, her hungry mind absorbing nourishment almost faster than she could digest it, her emotional hunger which, in her busy workaday life, she had failed to recognize in its disguise of unrest — gloriously satisfied by happy marriage, she still needed something. She rode in her beautiful new town-car and walked in the Park, she shopped with Aunt Lulie and lunched in restaurants at whose curtained windows she had once gazed in awe, she sewed a little and read a great deal; and still she wanted more, without knowing exactly what she wanted.

It was Aunt Lulie who brought the new need to a focus. Sitting comfortably relaxed in a deep chair, clad in a scarlet satin negligée in which she looked like a kindly lobster-for Mrs. John Leigh insisted on her presenting a severely chastened exterior to the world,

Gretel meditated on this suggestion. It was true that life in the apartment, with Aunt Lulie's loquacity and Kato's silence for her only social resources, was not thrilling. John, who worked like a trooper two-thirds of the days to spend the other third with her, asked nothing more of life; but she, who after all spent two-

thirds of her days waiting for him, needed some diversion. She did not want Society, but she did want more society than she had. 

And what was there to prevent her having it? She canvassed all possibilities of danger. Absolutely the only point of contact between her former world and the world she would enter as John's wife was Mr. Charlton; and she knew—not having private-secretaried him for two weeks in vain - that he spent his evenings at home with his invalid wife. She could emerge with perfect safety into an arena populated by total strangers, not one of whom knew any more of her than she of him.

Having found a new way to please her, he threw himself into the enterprise with his customary wholeheartedness. It was his habit to dump his invitations in an indiscriminate heap on the desk of his down-town office, where his secretary had been trained to dispose of them all with polite but uncompromising negations; but this time he himself called his prospective hostess on the telephone, told her to a volley of astonished squeaks that made him wince the news of his marriage, and asked permission to bring his wife to the party. Then he called up his foreman at the bridge, and made arrangements for being away another day. 

After that he took his new wife out and bought her an evening gown of cream-colored velvet and filmy lace, and a marvellous wrap of ivory brocade and soft dark fur, which he said was a post-wedding present, and a string of pearls, which he said was a pre-Christmas

present. She came in with her eyes a dark sparkle and her cheeks a red flame; and at sight of the new acquisitions Aunt Lulie gulped back so many tabooed ejaculations that she nearly choked. 

The theatre party began, for Gretel, in a sparkle of excitement. Their hosts whom they met in the lobby proved to be a heavy, florid man, and a blonde woman with bright geranium-red lips and a manner of weary vivacity; their fellow-guests were a smart, sophisticated couple by the name of Faulkner, a dark, sulky, handsome girl who had evidently been invited for John before Mrs. Miller had heard of his bride, and a spruce, sallow man with drooping eyelids who had been gathered in at the last minute for the sulky girl. 

They all stared at Gretel with a curiosity thinly veiled by conventional friendliness; and after the first glance the men stared more openly, and the women telegraphed each other, "She's stunning! Who in the world is she?" They all talked to her at once, except the sulky girl, who watched her narrowly in silence; and in the box where there was a good deal of bustling about, because they were two too many · they all maneuvered for a place near her. 

Gretel had never looked more lovely. Her warm, creamy skin repeating the hue and texture of her frock, her velvet-dark eyes shining with zest for life, her cheeks and lips brilliant with color, her dark head and slim, beautiful body erect and eager, she drew all the eyes in her neighborhood. None more irresistibly than 

John's. He tried, with perfunctory politeness, to make conversation with his hostess and the dark girl, as he found a place between them; but while he talked he gazed on his young wife with almost indecent absorp-

tion; and the eyes of Mrs. Miller twinkled appreciatively, and those of the girl narrowed satirically. 

The box was one of the two best, second from the stage on the lowest tier; the house was brilliant with light and color; and Gretel, who had never before seen a theatre from such a point of vantage, would have been content to do nothing but sit and look about. But she was too interesting an exhibit, both as a lovely young woman and as the unexpected wife of the elusive John Leigh, to be let alone. Everybody wanted to talk to her; and they all began—almost simultaneously—as soon as they were seated. She had seen the "Frivolities" repeatedly, in earlier editions; but always from far above, unreal and toylike. Now it burst upon her in the full blaze of its splendor, coruscating with

jewelled light, vibrating with many-voiced music; and as the brilliance and the pulse of it smote her senses, she kindled with excitement. Girls in raiment of exquisite textures, in raiment of jewels, in no raiment to speak of, moved and swayed before her, unfolding into huge fans, gathering into constellations; men sang and talked and danced, and were touching or absurd; musicians played intricate rhythms and harmonies; and she sat in a box, clad in lace and velvet and pearls- real pearls and it was all hers. Ellen James in the second balcony. Mrs. John Leigh in a box, wearing real pearls. Oh, what a gorgeous, incredible experience! Oh, what a wonderful, wonderful world! · · • • • She was so rapt in enchantment that when the end of the long act came, and the curtain fell and the lights of the house went up for the intermission, she looked about her in a momentary blank of bewilderment. Her neighbors in the box, bored by the lavish entertainment and alert to resume the more interesting topic interrupted by it, stared at her; they saw genuine enthusiasm so seldom that they did not recognize it.

She felt uncontrollably uneasy; and in the very act of acknowledging the introductions with a smile and a gay, pretty wave of the hand, she could not resist flinging a glance over her shoulder.

She must not, must not betray herself. She clenched her hands so tight that the nails bit the palms, and forced herself, still smiling, to turn back to Mrs. Miller and the clamoring neighbors.

Bunny's presence was easy enough to explain; he had always hated office work, and always talked about getting a job in a theatre. Bunny probably hadn't recognized her; and it was only because she was nervous and jumpy that she had found something sinister in Laura Brooke's glance. Lunch-time arrived, and, thanks to her vigilant defenders, not a disturbance except those engendered by her own fancy had come near her; the incursions dwindled, and finally ceased; and she draw a long breath of relief, and told herself she had been a fool. 

When neither the telephone nor the doorbell had rung for more than an hour, she was sufficiently at ease to try for a little of the sleep of which last night's uneasiness had robbed her. It was Kato's custom to go out during the afternoon, for marketing and for his own dim, mysterious concerns; but Aunt Lulie, who was at her apogee with a fire and a soft chair and a box of caramels for companions, mounted guard in the living-room and promised to repel all invaders. • • ·

XVIII 

Gretel's yielding to despair was not for very long. 

She stiffened her backbone, and took up her burden of fear with a sort of dogged gallantry. John, his love and his kind of life were a part of her now, and she must keep them at any cost.

So she went on living as if no cloud had appeared on the horizon-reading, seeing pictures and listening to music with a fierce determination to improve herself, driving out with Aunt Lulie in her beautiful car, making her person and her home ever more daintily attractive for John's coming. She bore herself with poise and calm, wore her clothes and her beauty with distinction, and drew her husband deeper and deeper into love; and 

Aunt Lulie, whose disposition when comfortable was always to let the morrow worry about itself, settled down again to the fire and the caramels.

But she found also as people usually do—that it is easier to start something than to stop it. The people she had met on her sortie into the world of fashion telephoned her, tried to call on her, invited her to festivities which, a little while before, she would have thought irresistibly alluring. Other people some of them obvious climbers and society-column rangers, some sincerely attached friends of John's-came forward eagerly with proffers of hospitality and friendship. To

them all she returned an unmitigated refusal, too riddled by secret fears to take any more risks: but they wouldn't be refused, they all persisted. She grew to hate the tireless trill of the telephone bell.

And the most difficult of all to deal with was John. 

Having once taken the step from the cloister into the outer world, and seen the impression his lovely bride made on its citizens, he was eager for more; he was not only earnestly desirous of making her happy, but, the main bugbear of his own social relations having been removed with his bachelorhood, he discovered a natural interest in his fellow-men. His work, which had once meagrely filled his days, now fitted into a richer and more normal life, and he was ready for his meed of pleasure. "Here's an invitation from the Langleys' for dinner," he would say, ploughing through the heap of accumulated mail. "Shall we go? They're delightful; you'd like them." Or, "Here's the third time in a week that

Mrs. Miller's asked us to do something. This time it's the theatre again. I thought you loved it before — ? " 

At last, driven from one excuse to another until they were all worn threadbare, she grew desperate and decided in mere self-defense to make another venture. 

Her former experience having left her with an unconquerable distaste for the theatre, and John's dislike for large affairs continuing in spite of his new social flowering, she screwed her courage to a dinner, given by some old and valued friends of his. John was as delighted as a child who has been promised a birthday party; and seeing his pleasure, she tried to banish her own misgivings and be gay with him.

The second sortie of the John Leighs into society be-

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

gan with a flare and a fanfare. Gretel had assumed that she would wear the creamy frock and pearls in which she had duchessed it at the theatre; but that would by no means do for John. He asked her if she had ever heard of a bride with only one evening gown, and called her, scathingly, a tightwad. In obedience to his behest, she went out to buy another, and returned with a modest little black lace which, as she told him, would do for any occasion. Then he arose in sudden might, whisked her out under his arm, and bought her a cherry-red velvet gown that billowed like a crinoline, silver slippers, and a ruby pendant on a thread-thin platinum chain, which he said would do for no occasion but one. "Now that'll show you," he said, "what I mean when 

I say get a dress! It'll show the world, too—that when

Mrs. John Leigh steps out they'd better shade their eyes!"

It did indeed show a number of things. 

For one thing, it showed Gretel as a vivid, dazzling beauty. She had dressed, ever since her purse turned into Fortunatus', with the utmost restraint, her fastidious taste shrinking from any ebullition of the nouveauriche; and had looked lovely, well-bred, and subdued. 

Now she flamed into such brilliance that she startled herself. She looked a wholly new person, a person too notable and arresting to be ignored for a moment. She enjoyed this flowering not one bit; it made her uneasy.

For another thing, it showed John-rather surprisingly as the primitive, proprietary male. He had always adored his Gretel, but as a wonderful discovery, as a rare and precious trust. Now, unexpectedly, he revealed the most ingenuous and surprising pride of ownership; he, so contemptuous of his own prowess and 

---

cc position, suddenly swelled like a little boy with his first watch. 'Just think of it!" he kept saying. "That belongs to me- all that!" It made Gretel laugh, but it added to her discomfort. She had never wanted him to be proud of her; there was too little cause.

For a third thing, it showed that when Ellen James, alias Geraldine, transformed herself into Mrs. John Leigh she did a far more momentous deed than she supposed.

She thought she was only gratifying an irresistible hunger of her heart; instead, she was arousing an answering hunger more keen and desirous than anything she had ever felt, and social complications far beyond her imagining.

She started for the Belknaps' house, in the beautiful frock and wrap and car, herself brilliant in beauty, her eyes shining with unhappy excitement, and her heart thumping with fear. "You look like the Spanish Infanta setting out to conquer the entire known world! " said John.

Gretel gave him a fleeting, unhappy smile. She felt more like a small and ill-protected spy, precariously invading a hostile country.

Mr. and Mrs. Belknap lived in a new-old, unpretentious and very expensive house close by the East River, in a part of the city which Geraldine had supposed to be almost as inelegant as Aunt Lulie's former haunts, and in which she was now surprised to discover an oasis of extreme smartness. The approach through down-at-heels streets like the ones she had grown up in eased her nervousness a little, but the green door opened by a butler, the downstairs cloak-room dominated by a uniformed maid, the broad stairway leading to spaciousness, light

Now that she was able to look at him, Gretel found him a droll person, with a long face and big ears, and something of the comedian's quality (she was shocked to find herself thinking), that Bunny had; but of course, she reflected, as he had been at Groton and at Harvard with John, it must be a different kind of comedy; and in truth through all his Merry-Andrew looks and manners a gentle quality showed indefinably. "La-dies and gen-tulmen! " he said. "I rise to propose a health-the health, no less, of the bride and groom. Gretel stood smiling, confused and beautiful, holding her glass in her hand. The commotion of the exodus to the dining-room and the finding of places around the table gave Gretel a chance to collect herself, and steady her trembling nerves; and when they were all seated, she discovered, to her relief, that Mrs. Faulkner was near neither herself nor John. She drew a long breath, and tried to give her mind to the enthusiastic conversation of her neighbors. 

She was seated between Mr. Belknap and a portly, comfortable young man whose place-card informed her that his name was Wainwright. They had both been classmates of John's, it seemed, from his schoolboy days, and neglecting their other neighbors they talked to her, antiphonally and in concert, about what a great old scout he was, how they had missed him since he had taken to the wilds, how glad they were to see him back again, and how they hoped she was going to make him stay. Listening greedily to their praises, she relaxed little by little and began almost to enjoy herself. ee "Oh, do go on!" she said artlessly, when one of them finished telling some tale of John's youthful prowess.

She had forgotten - though she herself possessed it—the faculty of quick-witted women for carrying on two conversations simultaneously and hearing every word of a third (indeed, there are many who can do this and play bridge at the same time), or she would never have permitted herself to feel safe even between her two kind neighbors. She was never safe. She never had been safe, for one moment, since she began this miserable game of deceit not even at home, not even alone with John. . . . XIX 

HIGHWAY ROBBERY 

After her second venture into the world, Gretel's nerve was so shaken that she could not bring herself to try it again. She was sure everyone knew by this time about her shameful imposture, and that if she appeared among John's friends nothing could keep him from knowing it too. John still wanted to exhibit her, she was still driven from excuse to excuse as the invitations poured in; and at last, growing desperate, she fell back on the plea of ill health. Her head troubled her; she wasn't sleeping well; she wasn't equal to meeting strangers, and wouldn't he please, please not ask her any more?

This resource worked like magic as far as protection from outsiders was concerned. All invitations were refused as inexorably as in the old days, and the vigilance of her guardians was increased until she could not have been safer in a jail: even Laura Brooke, who called three times on occasions when John was admittedly in town, was effectively excluded. But what peace she gained by this immunity she lost through its effect on John. His solicitude for her, always alert, increased to acute anxiety. He watched her constantly, with eyes whose troubled devotion she could hardly bear; he tried to prevent her from indulging in the most harmless and spontaneous exertions; it was only

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

by urgent pleading that she could dissuade him from calling a phalanx of doctors into attendance. For the first time since their meeting, she was glad when he had to go back to the bridge, and she could be free from his loving, torturing surveillance.

Then Bunny reappeared. 

She saw him first one morning when - John at his bridge, and Aunt Lulie snoozing by the fire-she was starting for a solitary walk. She had wakened in a brighter mood than she had known for days, relieved by John's absence and the remission of immediate anxiety, and buoyed up by the natural dependence of youth on the future. She returned the greeting of the elderly doorman almost gayly as she stepped out into the sunshine. After all, she told herself, she had been making bogies again; this was New York in the twentieth century; they couldn't break into her house with battering-rams, or hale her before an Inquisition. She needed only to keep her head and her courage, and she could keep her happiness too. . . . cc 

Good-mornin'!" said a voice at her shoulder. "Fine day fer the race, ain't it — human or hoss!"

She did not start, but her heart, which had just begun to flutter up into the sunshine, dropped like a shot bird. In her bedroom, she canvassed her resources. It was true that she had no money; and while she lived surrounded by luxury, not much of it was convertible into cash. All such things as were small and salable were gifts from John, which she loved, and whose absence he would be sure to notice. She looked them over; her pearls, the jewelled watch he had given her when he chose a birthday for her, the rings he had picked out with such loving care- they were all out of the question. The exquisite raiment which filled her cupboards, though it represented many hundreds of dollars, would be worth comparatively little at second hand. At last she gathered up a few trinkets — the mate of the gold bottle she had given Aunt Lulie, some earrings and shoe buckles she had bought herself at one of the shops where she had an account, a small alligator bag- and, more uneasy than ever, returned with them to the livingroom.

He seemed to have taken nothing but Gretel's peace of mind. But that had been a precarious possession, of late, at best.

XX 

Bunny's reappearance had the effect of crowding every inch of her world with the bogies whose existence she had been trying to deny. Now that she had definitely admitted, and succumbed to, the ugly possibility of blackmail, she seemed to herself to be definitely allied with crime. She lived in constant watchfulness, constant fear; she hardly dared go out of the door, or even look out of the window. Bunny and his shameful demands, the sulky girl and Mrs. Faulkner and their shameful knowledge, seemed always lurking around the corner; and everyone she met wore a knowing oblique look, as if aware of her secret.

Her preoccupation with imaginary danger soon had a very real effect. She lost all the sparkle that happiness had given her, and grew pale and haggard-eyed; she slept badly, and started at every sound. To hide her perturbation she tightened her rigid armor of self-control, not permitting herself to indulge any impulse or perform any action, however harmless, without first observing it from every angle to see how it might appear to the beholder; and her nervous tension increased until ease of mind or body became impossible to her. Wakeful by day and by night, rigidly wary all the time, she changed in a few days to a pallid, starting ghost. Her mirror hardly knew her for the radiant bride who had come into her kingdom only two short months before.

John was thrown into a fever of anxiety by the change-visible enough to any eyes, startling and appalling to his. He hovered over her with an anguished solicitude that added the last touch of torture to her state. Whatever she did, his troubled gaze was upon her; whenever she turned in her restless bed at night his vigilant voice demanded, "What's the matter, sweetheart?" She could hardly bear it; more than anything, she wanted not to be noticed. 

To make matters worse, Christmas chose this untoward moment to arrive. In anticipation a cherished joy, it turned out the ghastliest of festivals. John stayed three days, watching his wife with incessant apprehension. She decked the apartment with holly and mistletoe, purveyed gifts and feastings, and flogged herself into a ferment of false gayety which deceived nobody. The days were a misery, the nights a torment. 

When at last he tore himself from her - infinitely reluctant and she kissed him goodbye with pale lips, she felt that if he had stayed another minute she must have screamed at him. 

., And now came January, the stalest, most unprofitable month of the year; dirty snow in the gutters, abandoned Christmas-trees, with forlorn draggles of tinsel sticking in their branches, protruding from the ash-cans, nothing left to look forward to. January is bad enough at best; as the pessimistic Irish verse puts it, ce 

Christmas comes but wanst a year,

An' when it comes it brings good cheer. 

An' when it goes it laves us here, An' what'll we do for the rest o' the year?"

But when the external dreariness is complicated by fear and foreboding within, January is out-Januaried. The whole world seemed, to Gretel, squeezed flat beneath a load of despair. She had no longer the heart to read or to take her daily exercise; she sat by the window, stared at the dirty snow and the forsaken Christmas-trees piled on the street-cleaners' trucks, and wondered what was to become of her. 

Even Aunt Lulie yielded to the prevailing depression. 

Gretel's unease, John's manifest misery, the loss of her money at the hands of one whom she was ready to regard as a cherished nephew, and January, combined to give her a strong impression that all was not for the best in what she had so lately regarded as the best of possible worlds. The very strangers at the gates knew she was cheating him; the very name by which they called her was part of a monstrous fraud; and they knew it; and she was powerless to help it. Yes, she was powerless! She clenched her hands. She couldn't tell now, and ruin all their precious happiness, and break John's heart and her own. Every day made it more impossible. The antagoism at the root of the relation between these two had thrust its bare head up into the daylight, stripped of all veils of prudence and mutual self-protection. Both were candidly, vigorously angry, both pleasantly reckless of consequences.

Yet when, on Saturday, John came again, he throbbed with tenderness and devotion as always. He held her close in his arms; his love yearned over her.

Jenks would never rest until he knew the whole truth; the only mystery was that she should have left him in ignorance as long as this; and her breathless waiting for the blow to fall tightened her quivering tension almost unendurably. -- XXII 

In the morning John insisted that she should have her breakfast in bed, and while, resting luxuriously among her pillows still with that inner tension which never forsook her, be the pillows as soft as they might — she glanced through the morning papers, he went over his mail. Above her picture-sheet, through the open study door, her eyes kept seeking him. Like all people who deal successfully with many affairs, he was systematic; he sorted the letters rapidly into groups, one to leave behind with his secretary, one to take back with him to the mountains, and one to answer now. She admired the sureness of his movement, his rapid yet careful decisions. 

Everything he did had, for her, that satisfying quality of just-rightness which is part of being in love. 

Her thoughts drifted back across yesterday, darting here and there among memories and impressions like fish in a pool. . . . How miserable she had been before he came, how cold with fear — that was an icy depth of the pool, which made her shiver even in retrospect. 

How wonderfully he had come to her rescue; he had spoken almost brutally to Miss Jenks, in a hard, hating voice, because she abused his wife; he could be merciless in her defense. That was a warm upper reach of the pool, in which she basked gladly. . . . If only she could dwell all the time in this sunny security!

Faulkner. She offered some comment in her turn, and they both dissolved in malicious laughter.

corner, would they? Sneer at her within plain view of her eyes, and almost within sound of John's ears? She'd see about that. A glow of anger began to banish the icy chill from her heart.

Gretel, stabbed in her most vulnerable spot at the very moment of her triumph, was suddenly stricken deathly pale and silent. She answered him only with a little twisted smile.

XXIII 

THE WRECK 

There was nothing, absolutely nothing now to fear. 

She told over and over to herself all her guarantees of safety. 

The dark girl was effectively disposed of; she had shot her bolt, and failed. Miss Jenks was disposed of too; Dr. Hazelton, in his neutral way, was a sort of ally; Bunny would make no overt trouble. Indeed, no matter how many enemies there were, nothing that any of them could do could injure her. She was safe, protected from every danger by the perfect trustfulness of John's love. 

Then why wasn't she happy?

She went about with a hard, heavy lump in her breast, as if her heart had turned to lead. Or perhaps it was her breakfast; that seemed to turn to lead too, whenever she tried to eat one; it was as if her body had joined with her spirit in a determination to refuse the peace she so longed for. She felt wholly sick and wholly forlorn. "My Gawd, dearie," protested Aunt Lulie, "ya look as melancholy as a graveyard worm. Why'n't ya chirk up a little? Of course there was that aspect of the situation; 

John, who loved her so tenderly, had a right to his happiness. . . . She thought of the many motion pictures she had seen based on the theme, Should She Tell? 

In each of these the heroine, with much wringing of hands and heaving of bosom, had ended by telling all, and the hero, with much furrowing of brow and nobility of gesture, had ended by forgiving her. But it

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

had always been a matter of a concealed elopement or an illegitimate child, and Gretel knew well that a man of John's integrity would find it far easier to forgive a sexual slip than a course of deliberate deceit like hers. 

She herself found her own action the more unforgivable; sometimes she thought the reason she felt so ill was that even her body was in revolt against the thing she was doing. No, Aunt Lulie was right; John could never endure the knowledge of her treachery, and she must keep it to herself. She couldn't lose John. 

Better, far better, bear her burden alone all her days, no matter how it tortured her. She pinned her hopes to the journey they were about to take, and tried to absorb herself in it. But the days of preparation dragged heavily. John came and went; there were labor troubles at the bridge, and he was unable to leave until they were adjusted; and then his chief assistant fell ill; and then there was some new and unexpected plastic-surgery to be done to the stubborn old face of Nature. Gretel had fittings for her white clothes, and studied the steamship company's vivid pictures of enchanted lands, and walked about aimlessly, always carrying that leaden weight. Her hands fretted each other, her teeth nibbled her under lip; her impatience to be away mounted almost unendurably, and still had to be endured. 

At last, after the sailing-date had been twice changed, the day came on which they were really to start. Resuming her original name had brought pointedly to her attention the many changes that had come about since she bore it before, and this careful attention to duty was one of them; the first Ellen's chief idea in regard to her work had been to do it well enough to "get by," but the new Ellen was a sober, serious business woman. She thought it another retrogression; she was hardening into a machine, like Miss Heaton. Pretty soon even the boss wouldn't want to kiss her. Well, that would be one gain. Nowadays her way lay through the subway again the old familiar way of struggle, swelter, and smells. 

She jammed her hat on firmly, buttoned her coat tight, pinioned her bag under her arm and plunged into the maelstrom. 

She thought she had hated it before; but never, until being a millionaire's wife opened the shining doors of the world of luxury to her, had she known how loathsome it really was. When she stood at the top of the stairs, and the roar and the acrid whiff of it came up and smote her in the face, she staggered, and for a second something inside her said despairingly that she could not go on. But there's no use, she replied to it firmly, in saying you can't do a thing you know you have to do: so she set her teeth together, let herself be ground through the turnstile with the rest of the human sausage-meat, and battled her way into a north-bound train as sturdily as she used to do when she first was Ellen-of-the-office.

She and Aunt Lulie were living now on the east side of the city, in order to be near the College where she was taking lectures three evenings a week. There was no difference in aroma or in social atmosphere between the east-side subway and the west-side, but as she worked farther downtown than she used to the journey was longer, and as she had been riding for months in a cushioned limousine it was far more painful. She got out at Fifty-ninth Street, battered and weary, and trudged the last blocks of crowded pavements with what seemed the last dregs of the day's energy.

There were stairs again, of course. Down and about them flowed the stale and musty smells that had made up so much of the atmosphere of her life-onions, old tobacco, fried food, dust, all blended in one thick, complicated effluvium. The steps were bare, stained dark brown at the edges, worn in the middle to a dingy paleness; on their resounding surface the feet of the homing tenants scuffed noisily. Ellen's own feet were light, but they moved so wearily, and she lifted each one with such a constantly increasing sense of its weight, that they seemed to her to be beating out their ascent in a succession of dull thuds like the sound of a tom-tom. 

She had to climb four flights. "Flights!" she thought ironically, plodding upward.

When she reached the door she stood still for a moment, catching her breath and steadying her courage, before she took out her key. There would be no burned cabbage to face now, for Aunt Lulie's sojourn among the fleshpots had weaned her from cookery, and all their meagre dinners came from the delicatessen-shop around the corner; but the slump in domesticity had been accompanied by a greater slump in morale, and the homecoming was several degrees worse than it used to be. ce "Hello, Aunt Lulie!" she said, whipping up a spurt of spurious cheerfulness as she entered. ee Dinner ready?" cc

Ellen ate her meal with the steady courage which she had to bring to bear on every detail on her life now. 

It was always like this; complaints at home, annoyances at the office; fatigues, frets. In the old days she would have shrugged off as much of the dismalness as she could, and forgotten the rest at a dance or a pictureshow. But of late she had developed a stronge sense of responsibility. It was her fault that Aunt Lulie

was so discontented; she felt as if she had on her hands an unhappy fat baby, spoiled through her own mismanagement, curable only by her own patience; and the discomforts of the office were her fault too, since she had thrown away Mr. Charlton for a dishonest dream. 

However, she was not unaware that the incessant pinpricks of annoyance were a sort of cross-grained blessing, helping her to keep her mind off the great devastating ache which underlay everything she felt and did.

She washed her dishes and put them away neatly, and then went into the little bedroom to prepare for going out again. Aunt Lulie followed, the gold slippers groaning audibly at each step. HONEYMOON TRAIL 

She hurried out of the front door, and down the stairs. Traffic had subsided now; the streets were quieter, the sidewalks clear enough for comfort. She walked along Fifty-ninth Street until she came to Park Avenue, and then, in the spacious dignity of that exclusive thoroughfare, rapidly northward. Her spirits rose a little as she went; she was refreshed by her meal, it was a relief to escape from Aunt Lulie's repinings, and she was genuinely interested in the next undertaking. 

But always, under every thought and every action, lay that bitter, resolutely ignored, eternally unsleeping pain....

For half the month since she and John parted, she had been coming three evenings a week to this asylum of the city's little sisters. She had had no trouble in finding work, such as it was, but in addition to the spur of her native upward-looking ambition, she had an unsleeping inner urge to make herself more worthy of John. 

Not that she allowed herself any hope of ever seeing John again; she knew she had wounded him past forgiving; but he had loved her once, and she had that to live up to.

Girls of all descriptions were mounting the steps and moving to and fro in the spacious corridors-plump girls and lean girls, shabby girls and bargain-countersmart girls; they were various as Nature's lavish inventiveness makes any crowd of people, but yet they all looked alike, for each one was animated by an eager earnestness that overrode physical characteristics. They were all poor, these girls of the evening classes; they all worked hard in the daytime for the privilege of working hard again at night. Some came from factories, some from sweatshops; some were immigrants so new

ery that they spoke English only with an effort. Among them Ellen was an aristocrat, a creature borne on flowbeds of ease. She would have been ashamed to think of herself with pity here, even if she had had the will. 

Ellen was taking courses in English literature, history and French. She had chosen them only because they were prerequisite to a librarian's training, but now, to her surprise, she was becoming deeply interested in them for their own sakes. Tonight, at first, it was history.

It was her good fortune to have for a lecturer a man who was a gentleman as well as a scholar, who taught in the great university on Morningside Heights by day, and satisfied a secret humanitarian urge through these ministrations to the masses by night. He was a man of middle age, tall, slender and distinguished in person, with a keen, intellectual face and a manner of great charm. His thoughts came out clothed in words that were like himself— distinguished, stimulating, warm with humor and with sympathy. He lectured here exactly as he would have lectured at Oxford, giving the best there was in him. Probably he never even faintly suspected the rapture of delight that his trenchant intellect and his deferential courtesy stirred in the bruised, stifled young creatures who listened to him so avidly. 

Ellen, while urged into a whole new world of intellectual curiosities by his words, watched him with a throb of heartache. He reminded her of Mr. Charlton and of John — not that he was in the least like either, but because he belonged to the world in which they lived, toward which her homesick spirit was always yearning. Tonight, while he told the story of Nell 

Gwynn and her friend "Charlie Rex," his sudden charming smile lighting his fine face, John's chuckle

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

of appreciation sounded so audibly in the ear of her mind that she had to bite her lips to keep back a sob. 99 "Gee," breathed a swarthy, untidy young garmentworker who sat beside her, "I could die fer that guy! "I should think he was too old to fall in love with," returned Ellen, dispassionately. "In love with!" murmured the other, turning wide and horrified eyes to her as at the mention of a sacrilege. "Me in love with him?”

When the history hour was over, she had an hour of literature. This time her teacher was a woman, a member of the college's permanent staff. Like most modern professional women, she was alert, well-dressed and attractive; her hair was short and smooth, her eyes briskly alive in a bright, mobile face. She talked to the girls comfortably and informally, and each one felt as if she were conferring with a friend. "You must remember," she said, "literature isn't something finished and bound and set up on a shelf. 

It's as old as Cleopatra's needle over there in the Park, and as new as the 'Extras' the newsboys will be calling when you go out. Some of you will help make it. All of you can foster it. It's yours, just as much as it's 

Shakespeare's. Never forget that."

Ellen walked back through the night-chilled streets with a calmer and steadier spirit than she had had all day, so stimulated and refreshed that she didn't mind going home. There was something austerely good about life as she was living it now; it was dreary, but it was honest; its bitterness was wholesome and tonic. If she had little, that little she earned fairly. It was better, she said to herself, to be pulling your weight in a fisherman's dory than stealing a passage in a yacht.

She entered the flat quietly, and found that Aunt Lulie had taken herself and her sorrows to the embrace of Morpheus — where, apparently, the burden of them had become less intolerable, for she snored buoyantly. 

Ellen set the kitchen table for her breakfast, and put the milk-bottle on the dumb-waiter. Then she undressed in the living-room (she slept nowadays on the couch, having been unable to bring herself to such a pitch of resignation that she could go back to the bedroom shared with her aunt), and laid her clothes in neat readiness for the next day; for in the morning time was short and the tide of traffic long, and both waited for no girl. 

When she turned back the cretonne couch-covermade by herself, in the soft colors she had learned to love when she beautified John's home there was the bed she had smoothed so neatly before she went to work in the morning, waiting for her weariness. The sheets were coarse but clean, the blankets thin but adequate. 

It was something like her life, she thought; the least that one could get along with, yet enough. re ee 

Bare bones," she said to herself valiantly, are good. I'm satisfied." 

That must have been why, as she lay down between the coarse sheets and pulled up the thin blankets, she was suddenly seized by a shuddering tremor. She knew it only too well; it was the percursor of one of those emotional storms that had shaken her at intervals ever since she and John parted. They came with a helpless trembling like that of the old woman's house under the tempest, and mounted to an agony of uncontrollable weeping; and she dreaded and fought them with all her power, for they left her so drained and exhausted

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

that she had to flog herself to take up the morning's burden again. ... "I won't cry," she said, within set teeth. "I won't!

I like my life; it's a good life. I'm making it better. I'm going to succeed in it. . . . I must go to sleep; I've got to start earlier tomorrow. I was almost late today.. 

I tell you I won't cry! What have I to cry about? I've got a job, haven't I? The rent's paid, isn't it? I won't cry. I'm going to sleep." She slipped her hand under her pillow as she so often did at sleeping-time; and as she did so she remembered 

John's hand slipping in from the other side of the pillow to catch it. . . . John's voice calling it, in that broken husky tone of tenderness all lovers know, a little burrowing mole. . . . John's warm lips kissing it before they kissed her lips. . . . HARD GOING 

It had been the beginning of February when Ellen and John parted; and now it was near the end of 

March. Ellen had been working six weeks, and going to college four. She and Aunt Lulie were solvent, and part of their furniture was paid for; she had even been able to buy herself some shoes and stockings-"seconds" again, as in the old days. Her face was pale, her mouth resolute, and her eyes clear and steady, faintly shadowed underneath by olive rings. 

She looked quite different from the way she had looked half a year ago; with less of prettiness and more of strength, and somewhere beneath the surface of her young gravity a wistful hint of a new sort of beauty burgeoning.

In the Park the brownish wintry grass was brightening into that tender green that makes the experienced heart ache with homesickness for springtimes past, and the fledgling heart sick with yearning for lovely unknown things. The sheep were out grazing again, the iridescent ducks floated on the pond in their lazy, regal manner. Ellen, in whom the experiences of the past months had bred an almost intolerable sensitiveness to beauty as well as a wholly intolerable loneliness, had to give up her Sunday walks in the Park. She could not bear to look at that young green grass.

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

All this time she had seen no sign and heard no word, not only of her husband, but of any of her former acquaintances. There was little danger of John's friends crossing the path she trod now, and she had carefully avoided the people who had been her associates in her pre-John days. She meant to keep her life, such as it was, wholly in the present. Her one desire was to work as much and feel as little as possible.
