Ellen's sturdy bourgeois blood surged up in a warm, quick tide. She twitched her elbow out of his clutch, drew back, and administered to his pasty cheek a slap that sounded in the constricted space like the report of a Fourth-of-July pistol. Then, invigorated by the spur of anger, she ran up the stairs as fast as her feet could carry her.

But even before she had reached the end of the first flight, her blood began to cool and her step to slacken. 

What did an insult from a worm like Bunny matter?

The only thing worth heeding was what he had told her. 

She went more and more slowly, thinking of it, trying to realize it. She had no shadow of a claim on John; she had no right to his name; the dear pride of having been his wife was no longer hers. Now, indeed, it was all over. The last thing that could happen to hurt her had happened; she felt numb from head to foot, as if she could not suffer any more.

Standing outside the dingy door, staring at the broken bubbles in the varnish that looked like little craters of extinct volcanoes, she made a plucking gesture at her left hand. She had to pluck three or four times, because her fingers were so cold that they fumbled clumsily; but at last she succeeded in getting off the wedding-ring, the only thing of John's giving she had kept. She did not look at it even after she had pulled it off. It was better to keep staring at those little volcanoes-dead, like everything else in her life. Dead.

XXVI 

The days went by, as days do. Morning comes, and brings work; night comes, and brings sleep-sometimes; and at intervals one has to eat. It's cowardly to die on purpose.

So March ended, cold and dreary out-of-doors and inside the heavy heart; and there came a Sunday in April. "My Goddlemighty!" ejaculated Aunt Lulie, in the bedroom.

Ellen, in the kitchen, paid no heed; her aunt's pious invocations were frequent, and this one might mean that she had pricked her finger, read of the overthrow of the government, or decided that it was going to rain. 

She went on preparing the breakfast tray. Aunt Lulie's dejection had reached appalling depths, and Ellen was trying to lighten it by the offering of a luxurious morning in bed. She welcomed the slight extra labor entailed; she made a point of filling all her waking hours with small activities, crowding them close together so as not to have one moment free for suffering. Yet continually, over and under the small businesses, flowed the merciless stream of thought dark with unhappiness, flushing here and there with a remembered joy almost too vivid to be borne. 

"One moment, madame," she said, "and madame

I couldn't stay away from him, if he hated me a hundred times worse than he does." Now she was putting on the frock and hat in which she had first gone away in search of him, last autumn. If she put them on featly, it was because of that natural precision which made all her movement deft and sure; she had no thought of how she looked, or of anything but her desperate need of haste. "When ya gonna clean up that mess?" demanded her aunt. "I'm not going to clean it up at all," answered Ellen, ruthlessly. "It'll give you something to do when I'm gone." She slipped into her coat, and snatched up her gloves and the handbag into which she had thrown a few necessities. "Good-bye, Aunt Lulie!" she called as she went. "I'll write you, some time! " "Hold on!" cried Aunt Lulie triumphantly. "Where ya gonna get the money?' 

دو 

Money! always the grim stumbling-block of the poor. It was strange that she had forgotten about it; except for those few unreal months of luxury, she had never done anything in her life, since she grew old enough to be aware of what she was doing, without having to stop and think of it first. But it didn't matter; money or no money, she must go to John. "I'll get hold of some" she said, from the door, "I'll send you some, too, as soon as I can - can't stop to talk about it now 

good-bye!" 

She put her mind on it while she ran down the stairs. 

She had nowhere near enough for a journey, of course;

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

it was the first of the month, and she had paid the rent and started buying Aunt Lulie's phonograph. But she had one resource- her wedding-ring. In these last bitter days, since she had known her marriage for a shabby sham, she had questioned her right to the possession even of that; but she could not bring herself to part with it, whether it belonged to her or no. Now she knew she had been right to keep it; it was the only thing that could take her to John.

Hurrying down Third Avenue toward the nearest pawnshop, she slipped her hand inside her frock and drew it out. It was a narrow band of platinum, so delicately inset with diamonds that it looked like a tiny ribbon of frost; but it was not cold; it had been kept warm by her heart's blood ever since John put it on her finger. She thought of an old-wives' tale once told her by an ancient Irish crone in some crowded apartmenthouse of her girlhood; if a ring has never been allowed to grow cold, if a heart which beats with love has always warmed it, it has the power to awaken love whenever placed on the beloved's finger.. If she were to come near to John- if she could find him sleeping, so that he would not draw away from her in horror and slip the ring on his finger, would he wake up loving her again? ... No use wasting time on a lovely thought like that; without the ring she couldn't get to 

John at all. She went quickly inside the pawn-shop, whose charitable doors were open as wide on a Sunday as a week-day, and held it out—a flashing, frail last hope. Smiling cynically (your pawnbroker is society's chief cynic; he has seen wedding-rings pawned before), the dingy man behind the counter turned the delicate thing

in his thick fingers, peered at it through his little magnifying-glass, and gave her a yellow ticket and seventyfive dollars. It was not a large price for two hundred dollars' worth of delicate craftsmanship and a million dollars' worth of dreams; but it would do. 

Unable to bear the slow, interrupted progress of the street-car, she took a taxi to the station. At every delay her hands clutched the slippery cushions, and her body strained forward to push against the detaining obstacle. Oh, hurry, hurry, taxi-driver! Is it nothing to you that John lies broken and suffering, a cruel long distance away? Hurry, hurry, hurry!

But when she reached the station it appeared that there had been no need of haste; for there was no train. 

The one she had travelled by last September ran at a different time in the winter, and on Sundays did not run at all. The realization made her wring her hands; if she had to spend a whole day in inaction, she felt she would go mad. Besieging the informationbooth, explaining, with a voice of desperate appeal, that she must go, she enlisted the aid of one of its acolytes; and he flipping a practised finger through a sheaf of time-tables- laid out a devious route for her; New 

York to Albany, Albany to Gloversville, Gloversville to 

Brimstown, Brimstown to Craddocks' Falls. It would take all day long, but at least she could start at once.

That was something. She was on her way to John whatever she might find at the journey's end.

The journey was interminable. 

At first she occupied herself with a feverish search of all the papers she could buy, for news of John's accident. 

They all featured it if John Leigh in health was a

definite point of interest, John Leigh injured was invaluable - but the accounts were practically identical. 

He had gone down over the edge of the ravine to observe the progress of the bridge, had lost his balance, and had pitched to the bottom; had suffered a fractured skull and possible internal injuries; his condition was precarious. That he lived at all was due to the fact that the frozen bed of the stream was packed with snow; the other victims of the dangerous crag, who had struck on the naked rocks, had been killed instantly. Then followed the story of the Lovers' Leap, and of the fatalities which had made it memorable. She skipped that part, remembering with anguish the night when he had told her the tale, in the happy little storm-shaken room. 

Some of the papers carried pictures of the gorge, and of the unfinished bridge. These made her shiver. All of them made mention of the fact that John had married "Miss Gretel Vail," and one of them said, "The news of Mr. Leigh's romantic and mysterious marriage aroused much interest a few months ago. Mrs. Leigh is not with her husband at present, and it is believed that she prefers travelling abroad to living in this country." This made her drop the sheet with an impatient exclamation. Imagine, if there were such a person as Mrs. John Leigh, her preferring to be anywhere else than where Mr. John Leigh was! "I'd rather live on an ash-heap with John, than in the royal suite of the royalest palace in Europe, with the royalest king in the world!" she said to herself.

When she had read all there was to read about John, she cast aside the papers, and sat staring out of the window.

It was one of those raw, rainy days with which early

spring gladdens our northern latitudes; gray mist over the fields, gray rocks darkened with moisture, gray trees only faintly beginning, here in the chilly uplands, to bud with spring promise. Her thoughts drifted drearily about, like drab ghosts in a vast windy void. . .

CLIMBING 

Inside, the shack had already been transformed with that bleak efficiency which characterizes the medical profession-into the semblance of a hospital. 

Whatever was superfluous had been banished; screens had been contrived out of white sheeting hung over some improvised framework; a strong scent of antiseptics, faintly permeated by the acridly sweet breath of ether, was in the air; and on the narrow cot, swathed and helmeted in white, his eyes closed and his face a ghastly brownish pallor, lay John.

When once she had seen him, Ellen had eyes for nothing else. Dropping her bag, she brushed past the astonished nurse and went straight to the bed, drawn irresistibly by that still white body and thin brown face. 

She gazed for a moment, with a passion of longing that might almost have penetrated the closed eyelids, and then fell on her knees and put her lips on the lax brown hand lying along the coverlet. ee " John — dearest!" she whispered, low to herself and to him.

The nurse, who had at first been too taken aback by this unceremonious entrance to protest, regained her mastery of the situation. "Pardon me," she said, coming forward with a scandalized face," but really, we can't have this! Mr.

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

Leigh's in a very critical state; no one but the physicians and nurses are allowed to see him; and really, for you to come in like this - we can't allow it, really!" 

Ellen looked up at her humbly. "I won't disturb him. I won't make any noise at all. I just want to to look at him. Please let me stay."

The nurse shook her head, kindly but decidedly. "I'm afraid not. He may regain consciousness any minute, and the slightest shock might be dangerous really."

At this Ellen rose swiftly; she wouldn't be a shock to 

John for all the world, even though seeing him was all that the world could give her. "Then I'll go," she said. "Tell him-" But there was nothing she could tell him; the very thought of her would be a pain in itself. She turned away, mute and stricken.

Something about the forlornness of her movement got beneath the nurse's professional surface, and she turned too, looking after the visitor. "Is there any message you'd like to leave?" she asked. "Shall I call you up when he comes to? "

Ellen shook her head. She could not remind him of the existence of a person who had never done him anything but harm; and there was no use asking to be called, since she had no idea where she could go. A sudden tightening of the throat, born of her helpless misery, made her unable to speak. She shook her head again, forlornly, and went softly out.

When she had closed the door behind her, she stood still, gazing into the wet, cold dark. She had never felt so lost and homeless in all her life. to do? They wouldn't let her couldn't, she couldn't go away!

283 time was even worse than leaving him before; she had the feeling that she was abandoning him to the creeping advance and stealthy pounce of Death. When she left him this time, she would never see him again. 

The thought was more than she had strength to bear. 

She crept along the rough side of the hut, under the dripping eaves, to the window, and stood gazing in. 

She could see the bed, and John lying in it, so still, so pale and unaware. In all the flush of health and love he had never been so dear as now. Her heart leapt into her mouth, and then as if it had been the lump in her throat that choked her it seemed to rush out in a great sob. "Oh-oh-John!" She clung to the window-frame, so racked and shaken that she could hardly stand, crushing in the agonized sound of her weeping with the back of her hand.

The whirr of a motor arriving and stopping in the muddy road startled her to a sense of her surroundings; and seeing a man emerge and walk briskly up the path, she stepped into the shadow, holding her breath. When he had entered the hut she peeped through the window again, and recognized that ruddy, genial Dr. Saunders whom John had called to her at the beginning of her deception. The memory of his kindness, and of the confidence John had in him, gave her a flash of hope.

Perhaps he would have a little pity for her. She would ask him for it; she was far beyond pride. She began to fight her sobs and to dry her eyes, mustering all the composure she could scrape together. 

Dr. Saunders looked at John -who lay as inert and unmindful as if he had been some lifeless fossil from a bygone age-talked a little with the nurse, and turned away; there was evidently nothing for him to do.

By the time she had returned from the stumbling journey into the chill dark, and set her full bucket triumphantly in its corner, the atmosphere had lightened considerably. The nurse let her natural kindliness triumph over curiosity and suspicion, and smiled her gratitude without reservations. They talked more easily, and were silent with less discomfort; and when there was something to be done, they did it together, companionably.

Emboldened by her progress, Ellen began to plan a further step. As the minutes passed, and the sounds of the men returning from their Sunday outing died away into silence, and the ticking of the clock sounded louder than ever, she kept a sharp eye on the nurse; and presently she discovered the watering eyes and forced smile of drowsiness, and then a life-sized, unmistakable yawn.

291 you know you need the rest! I'll stay close by him, and call you right away if he should wake up. Go on into the other room, and get a nice nap." We-ell, perhaps for just a few minutes - 

When she had successfully bundled the nurse out of the room and on to John's old leather couch, Ellen came and stood by the bed. At last, for a little while, he was hers all hers. Her heart lifted and swelled until she seemed lifted up by it, out of her wretchedness into a moment of pure ecstasy. Very lightly she touched his cheek; instead of being cold, as she had feared, it was warm with life. Throbbing, she knelt and laid her lips upon it. She stroked his hair; she put her arms softly around his shoulders, and cherished him with fearful, rapturous tenderness, as a mother cherishes her newborn baby.

Now the clock ticked soothingly, benignantly; the little stove purred kindly; the nurse snored gently, with a recurrent, reassuring sound. Ellen sat on the floor, her cheek on her husband's quiet hand. The floor was very hard, and as the night wore on, her body ached and stiffened with weariness. Yet when she thought of moving to a more comfortable spot, even her tired flesh joined her spirit in rebellion. All the long hours of the night she sat there, her cheek against the dear hand, her heart comforted. 

For the last time. 

Never mind that; never mind anything, now. . . .

XXVIII 

HIGHER 

"Mrs. Leigh," said Dr. Saunders, meeting her outside the door of John's shanty, his round, kind face very grave, "I have some rather disturbing news for you."

Ellen felt herself quiver with one of those premonitory pangs women know so well; but she forced herself to stand still and speak quietly, instead of clutching him and crying out her fear. "What is it, Dr. Saunders?” "John has recovered consciousness — " 

Yes, yes, she knew—and his first words had been, "Don't let her come near me!" She had gone through it all, in imagination, during that night of happiness shot with anguish. She held her breath and clenched her hands in her determination to take it without flinching. "But not memory. His mind's a blank. He doesn't know me, and I'm afraid he won't know you." 

Incredulous joy surged up through her panic. He wouldn't know her- then she could stay! She wouldn't need to flee away like a thief, without another sight of him! She had to press down almost as hard to stifle her unseemly relief as she had done to stifle her dread. "It's not a dangerous symptom, is it, doctor? asked, with an anxious afterthought. 99 she

293 ee "No. It's not uncommon after such a serious accident as his, and people usually come out of it without eventual harm. I only wanted to prepare you for a disappointment." "As long as he's not worse, I don't care," averred 

Ellen, very truthfully. "May I go in?" ee ee وو

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

with fierce tenderness; and she dropped again on her knees and held the hand close between both of hers. 

John's haggard eyes gave her a long look, and closed contentedly. There was a faint smile on his lips.

Now followed a strange time for Ellen.

When John slept - he slept a great deal; his bruised and broken body needed rest, which the mercy of opiates provided him-she suffered. She suffered because he was ill and broken, and she could not heal him; because his restoration to health meant that she must lose him forever; because the nurses could help and comfort him, and she was only a useless piece of furniture to be moved about out of their way. She suffered constantly, torturingly.

When John was awake, she had no time to suffer; she was too busy loving him. From the first opening of his eyes he clung to her, hungrily, needfully; and her love hovered over and enfolded him. She fed him, talked to him softly, sat beside him and stroked his hands; and the drawn look that was on his face even when he slept went away, leaving it wistfully content. Then she was so happy that her heart lifted up within her, and all her being was flooded with light like a room opened wide to the morning. 

Always - whether he slept or woke, whether she slept or woke she thought of him. She did not think of her work, or of how she was to live in the future; she did not even think of Aunt Lulie, after she had sent most of her money back to the abandoned hearth. She had the feeling that she was living in a moment of time isolated from all history, belonging to her and John alone. Behind it was misery; before it was black empti-

HIGHER 

297 ness; but this ineffable moment was hers, and she hugged it desperately.

S As the days passed, however, and the first marvel of being near him at all faded in the fact of her continued nearness, she began to scheme for a greater degree of possession. She hated sharing her treasure. Since he was hers only for a brief time, she meant to have him completely while she might.

To that end, she set to work to eliminate the nurses. 

The night nurse was easy; John required so little attention that the doctor-with whom Ellen's influence was now very potent was readily persuaded to let her go. That gave Ellen, as the new incumbent, an opportunity to establish herself in the back room of John's shanty; and encouraged by success, and feeling her position greatly strengthened by possession of the premises, she moved briskly on toward the day nurse's post. First she persuaded Miss Green, on a hygienic basis, to sell her some white uniforms, so that she subtly suggested the part; then, by careful observation and increasing practise, she learned to do all the things that had to be done for John; and all the time she kept familiarizing the doctor with her new status by being continually at hand, standing beside his elbow and ministering deftly to his needs. In a few days he took her as much for granted as Miss Green, and asked her for scissors and gauze and necessary services with as much confidence in her reliability. After that the expunging of the day nurse required only a little coaxing; and then Ellen reigned supreme.

She felt like a spurious queen who, by successful plotting, has come into a coveted kingdom. The very

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

knowledge that her power must be brief made her clutch it all the more greedily. She begrudged the toll of sleep that Nature demanded of her; half a dozen times a night she slipped from her cot to hover over her treasure, and make sure it was still there.

As for John, there was no doubt of his satisfaction with his new sovereign. His eyes sought her constantly; her name was the first word his forgetful lips said readily; his face lighted eagerly whenever she came near, and clouded with disappointment when she moved away from him.

She watched him narrowly for signs of returning memory. But the veil of oblivion was thick, and he seemed to be making not the slightest effort to pierce it. He was content to lie as much at ease as his battered body would let him, watching her, waiting wistfully for her to come to him, eating patiently but without pleasure the food she brought him. His mind, which used to be so alive and forceful, was now as supine as his body. He accepted whatever she taught him with unresisting docility, but sought no knowledge for himself.

Only in the one direction, his desire for her presence, did he show any initiative. Here she was both delighted and alarmed to observe an increasing alertness. In a few days he learned to call her when she was out of his sight; and soon after that he began to show curiosity about her movements. And suddenly with an intensity that made her ashamed even while it tingled through her in a wave of new life a hope flamed up in her; the hope that memory would never come back to him again, and he would be hers always.

XXIX 

When Ellen looked outside the shanty, she wondered what had become of the golden world through which she and John had journeyed last autumn. It was very cold. Spring, that by now was walking with assurance in the lowlands, only tiptoed about the skirts of these high places; the waterfalls were leaping, and the stream at the bottom of the ravine rushed along in spate, but the trees were bleak and bare, and in the clefts and hollows the hard-packed snow still lingered. It was very ugly, too; the bridge-building activities had scarred the face of the adjacent earth; and where there used to be forest loveliness, all was ravaged and raw. 

No one would have recognized the romantic beauty of the 

Honeymoon Trail. 

But she had no time to worry about the outside world. 

She had never, even in the days of the longest subway trips and heaviest overtime at the office, worked so hard. All the time that John was awake she was beside him, giving him the scrupulous care required by his physical condition, and the companionship demanded by his growing love. When he slept she had food to prepare, rooms to clean, clothes to wash. 

Her own sleep she took in snatches, ready always to jump like a fireman at the faint call of "Ellen!" She grew very thin, and the camp doctor-a gruff, shaggy bachelor who viewed women with contempt, but who had never-

theless a kindly eye for her-insisted on her having one of the laborers to do the heavy carrying; but except for the wood and water, every burden borne and every task done inside the shack was hers. 

She loved it. She looked at her hands - chapped and roughened in spite of all the care she took to keep them smooth for John- and wondered how soon, dainty and manicured again, they would be taking a letter in a comfortable office. And at that thought she shivered.

Dr. Saunders, who came up from the city twice a week, telephoned that he was bringing Dr. Hazelton for a consultation.

She had expected it, and feared it. John's mind was not clearing as fast as Dr. Saunders had hoped, and he had asked her permission to call a specialist. It seemed tragi-comic to her that he should consult her as if she had any rights in the matter; as if she were not as much an outsider as the Italian who fetched the wood. But she had consented soberly, keeping up the pretense of being Mrs. John Leigh which was the only excuse for her presence.

Now she was awaiting the visit with miserable forebodings. She felt at ease with the rough but reliable

Dr. Jones; he knew nothing about her, and so he trusted her. Dr. Saunders, who knew a good deal, and might well have suspected more, made her a little uncomfortable; but he had been very tolerant and kind, and she felt her tenure fairly safe under him. But Dr. Hazelton-whose keen eyes had known her for a sham from the very first glance, who had watched her through all the ignoblest part of her deception- what would he say to her? He would turn her out, of course, and small

Then she would go away: and that would be the end.

Alas, in how few hours would she need no help at all from Alessandro -or anyone else but her defeated self! The tears flooded her eyes again at the thought; and to hide them, and to indicate that the interview was ended, she walked away quickly to the end of the path.

Here, where the rutted lane that led to John's shanty debouched on the road, she was within sight of the bridge; and, trying to steady herself for the approaching ordeal, she blinked her eyes clear and concentrated her attention on it. She might never, she told herself drearily, have a chance to look at it again. 

Seen without prejudice, it was not a thing of beauty.

It was nearly across the gorge now, hanging, with a shocking effect of unease, unfinished in mid-air; for some reason not known to her, it was painted in three colors - the end nearest to her black, the next portion a dark and sinister green, and the rest a raw, ugly red. 

At the farther extremity a steam riveter lifted and banged with a merciless rhythm. During the months of her happiness with John, last autumn, she had learned that to him the bridge was something beautiful and purposeful, progressing by laws as orderly as those of Nature to a predestined end; and at that time she too could have looked kindly at it. But now she viewed it as the cause of his broken body and the anguish she was suffering, and it looked to her monstrous and menacing. ee

The days passed; and Spring climbed the mountains; and John's body, with its lifelong habit of health, grew steadily stronger; and Ellen held her breath. 

These were the sweetest days she had ever known; even sweeter than the early days of marriage. Yet they were the most torturing. Today John was hers utterly, to care for and cherish. Tomorrow he might be worse than a stranger to her. This very night-this very minute the change might come. She feared for her happiness more sharply with each tick of the clock; but she could not control it; it kept mounting and soaring. " Ellen! " John's voice was growing stronger. It may be true that the kiss is an artificial and conventional gesture, and that Hottentots prefer to show their affection by rubbing noses; but if so, John had a natural gift for artifice; he returned the kiss with a weak but unmistakable enthusiasm that made her blood tingle, and asked for the name and a repetition of the experience immediately. In a surprisingly short time it had grown into a part, not only of his vocabulary, but of his favorite practise. 

Ellen bent down to put her lips on his, gladly; but in a moment she raised her head with a startled look.

There had been a difference about this kiss. Usually she kissed him like a mother, and he responded with that gentle, infantine compliance of his; but today his lips clung to hers with force and fervor, in the unmistakable love-kiss of a man to a woman. An electric shock quivered through her body, ecstatic and breathsnatching; and at the same time her dread moved in her, like a child almost ready to be born. 

As if of their own volition, John's arms lifted—

with their clumsy, fumbling gesture of members long unused and went around her shoulders. At first they hardly seemed to know their own intention, but once arrived, they showed no more indecision. They closed about her in a warm, purposeful embrace, and drew her down, as they so often had done when they were John's own arms, until the two pairs of lips met again. 

When that kiss ended, Ellen's was not the only face that was startled.

Sometimes the curtain seemed to take the shape of a face that was pressed against it, trying to see through. Sometimes it blew so far that a glimpse of the face appeared at the side, peering out. But that was only for a second, the briefest flash; then it hung blank and motionless again. ec 

Ellen," he begged on a morning of bright spring sunshine, "kiss." He said this very often; he remembered the word much better than "broth" or "thermometer." 

Ellen bent over him from above not kneeling and brushed his lips with a light and fugitive touch.

She was afraid of kisses; it was they that seemed most potent to draw aside the curtain. She tried to straighten

up as soon as she had given her butterfly caress, and go away about her duties. But his arms came around her shoulders - more surely and strongly than they had done as yet and drew her down; and his lips, seeking purposefully, claimed hers for a thoroughgoing, workmanlike kiss.

Her heart stopped beating. He knew her! he remembered his love for her! Then in another instant he would remember his hate; she would see those eyes, which had grown soft with tenderness, harden into the dreadful look of aversion they had given her when they last saw her consciously. She could not bear it—yet; she must have a little time to gather her strength, after this breath-snatching shock.

She rose quickly, got the soothing medicine which the doctor had prescribed for restless nights in the weeks lately past, and gave him a dose. Then she darkened the room, smoothed the bed, and, drawing up a chair, sat beside him and stroked his head with the light, slow, unvarying motion that had never failed to soothe him. 

He looked at her in puzzled wonder for a moment, and made an effort to take her stroking fingers in his; but she put his hand gently down beside him in the bed, drew the covers over it, and went on with her stroking; and the question receded from his eyes, and the lids, (pale and delicately blue-veined, like a child's), dropped over them, and he was asleep.

As soon as she dared leave him, she sped to the telephone and called Dr. Hazelton's office. The doctor talked with her himself, for he was keenly interested in this case no less in the nurse than in the patient. She asked him to come up as soon as he could manage it, and he catching the undertone of frightened urgency in her voice-replied with a prompt assent. 

It was night when he arrived. John was asleep again. 

Ellen had dimmed the light with a shade of brown paper; the room seemed all shadows. Dr. Hazelton gave one of his penetrating glances to the quiet man in the bed, and one to the white girl beside him, and then turned toward the door. "Let's talk outside, Mrs. Leigh," he said. "Your husband doesn't want us now, and you'd be the better for a little fresh air yourself. Where's your coat? "

She got it from its peg and slipped it on, and they sat down on the doorstep, leaving the door ajar. The night was still and clear, with a moon; the pine-trees made their soft murmur, that never ceased, even when there seemed to be no wind blowing; that at first had seemed to Ellen dreary and troubling, but nowadays laid a soothing spell upon her. "Kiss, Ellen,” said John. ce

That's more important. Up on your feet-march!

He obeyed her willingly. Walking was a new accomplishment, and it intrigued him almost as much as kissing. He had been practising it for two days, two or three times a day, and each time more eagerly and strongly. He could walk all about the shack now, without any help from Ellen's shoulder; he could even look up at her as he walked, and smile proudly and happily. "Look, Ellen!" he said. "I walk alone! " 

His smile brought a lump into her throat. She couldn't bear it much longer. For that reason she was hurrying her work with desperate determination, to get it finished while she still had strength. 

That the walking had not, as yet, produced the result she was working for was no fault of hers. She was trying very hard. First she had carefully staged a surprise glimpse of his old work-room, where she now slept -effacing every trace of her occupancy, arranging his desk as it had been when she arrived, and then opening the door on it suddenly. But he glanced at it with oblivious blankness, and turned back at once to her. The next day she had drawn the window-curtains, got him

HONEYMOON TRAIL 

out of his bed in the dimness, led him to the window, and abruptly uncovered the vista of mountains- those mountains which had surrounded him so long-clothed in spring-color, the most poignantly reminiscent color in the world. But his eyes roved over them indifferently and without recognition, and came homing eagerly to her. Then she had tried a more ambitious venture; seating him in his accustomed chair, she had flung the door wide to disclose his old working-mate, the construction foreman, who at her signal came hurrying up the path as if for an urgent consultation. But that too had failed. John had frowned with mild annoyance, and said, "Doctor? I don't want doctor; I want

Ellen." His eyes could or would see nothing, it seemed, but her. 

However, Ellen was not beaten; she had another and a surer scheme. She knew what he loved best in the world-his bridge. She thought he had always loved it better than he had her; certainly he would do so when he remembered all the bitterness in which they parted. She was going, this day, to confront him with the bridge; she was going to wait until she saw recognition and knowledge creeping back into his eyes; and then, before they had time to remember her and look at her with hate, she was going to slip away forever. "Now, kiss!" coaxed John, when he had made the circuit of the room and was back in his chair again. "No, no kiss now. Do you know what we're going to do? We're going to walk out-doors!" "Out-doors?" "Yes; out there where the sunshine is. We're going to see something very nice-something you love." "I love Ellen."

His refrain was like a cuckoo-clock; but it didn't seem wearisome to her. Seeing him sitting there, his big body relaxed and powerless, his eyes yearning toward her adoringly, she felt her strength pour out of her like water. If she didn't do it now, she couldn't do it at all. 

She got his clothes from behind the canvas curtain that made the room's only closet, and brought them to him; and, puzzled but docile, he submitted to being dressed - even helping a little in a fumbling, mechanical way, as if his fingers had remembered stray bits that his mind forgot. The clothes hung loosely on him, and made him look thinner and more ill than he had looked in his pajamas and dressing-gown. -Oh, oh, to leave him now, while he had so little strength to face the world with! Perhaps just a few days more -? . 

Coward! which was it who really had not strength enough? ee "Now," she said briskly, we're going to have an adventure. We're going to see the Wide World."

Opening the door wide, she led him out, and he stood in the path, blinking. It was a radiant morning; one of those clean spring mornings that seem to be made all of blue and green and gold. The mountains loomed vast and near against the pellucid sky, and the sunlight poured through the new-leaved trees in a brilliant flood. 

The real John would have gloried in it; she remembered how, during their honeymoon, he had loved the great mornings of mountain splendor. But the sick John, who had lain so long in the shadows, shrank from it with a sort of dread. "I don't like the Wide World," he said uneasily. "I want to go there." He turned back toward the shanty.

She told him to keep his eyes down, and he obeyed her in his docile way until she halted him at the spot where the path joined the road that led to the bridge. Then she stepped back quickly out of his line of vision, turned him, and made him face the ravine. "There!" she exclaimed. "Now look!"

In front of them, stark in the clear light, stood the bridge. It was empty and quiet, for this was Sunday, and the men had scattered to their various domesticities or diversions; Ellen had chosen the time carefully, so that no distraction should confuse her experiment. The long, strong lines, the powerful machinery and heaps of strewn material-so meaningless to her, so informed with significance and value to his normal vision were spread before him like a picture constructed for him alone; and on this picture, and the making of it, he had looked daily for weeks and months; it was his own creation, as a painting or a book or a child might be. 

She held her breath. Now she would see remembrance flash across his face- and now her hour had struck. But he looked blankly at the clutter, at the gaunt skeleton and at the height beyond it, and then brought his eyes back hurriedly, as if distance hurt and tired until they rested on her that life them. 

It was not kindled in them again.

Couldn't she give up now let go, take him back to the quietness that was all he wanted, soothe him, and cherish him, and keep him for her own? . . . No more struggle, no more anguish; a little of the kindly drug that brought sleep, a little stroking of the hands. 

But another look at the bridge steadied her. To exile thought from a mind that could create a thing like that, to keep in the status of a child a man who had so fairly earned a man's place in the world, would be a crime worse than murder. Ellen was in despair. She couldn't give up now; if she did, John's future and her own integrity would be utterly demolished. It was cruelly hard to have to fight him as well as herself, but she had no choice; at any cost she must carry the thing through. But how?

She thought desperately. The bridge had conveyed no meaning to him, because he had never really seen it.

She would leave him there, and send the doctor to take him home. This was the end of happiness; the end of life. When she had finished this task, she couldn't go on; there was nothing left. And all at once she thought of the Lovers' 

Leap as a refuge. The snow that had saved John was melted, and she could hear death rushing, violent and sure, at the bottom of the gorge. One swift plunge too swift for thought or feeling—and all the anguish would be over! John would never miss her; in a few minutes he would be his old self, to whom Ellen was a breath on a mirror and Gretel an ugly memory. 

One swift plunge, and his returning happiness would be safe forever from the sight of her . . . and she would have done with heartache . . . forever. . . The thought came to her with a dark and dreadful comfort. For John, the beneficent shock that would recall his errant faculties and bring him a new, sane life; for her, the swift oblivion, and no more pain. This time, however, he was better prepared. Her resistance, instead of daunting him, whetted his appetite, and he came lunging after her. When the Honeymoon Trail should be ready for the cushioned tires of the honeymooners, the abyss would be defended by a wall, the road reach over to its new steel bed in a sleek unbroken ribbon; but now there was a wide gap between the path and the framework, and the footing was precarious in the extreme. Alessandro, with his broad feet and his habituated knowledge of the spot, had an immense advantage over her, whose small shoes were treading it for the first time. 

She slipped on some treacherous fragment of rock, and fell heavily against him; and he seized her in his arms.

Looking behind her, at the place where his terrified eyes were staring, she saw John -running, stumbling, plunging over the rough ground; oblivious of danger, visibly strong with the wild strength of rage. The gentle, blank face he had worn of late was distorted and terrible; the limp hands were knotted into fists. The sight of him struck terror even to her heart; and to Alessandro, who had seen him lying so long weak and impotent, he must have seemed a dread miracle or portent- the Big Boss come to life as an avenging god.

The man's arms dropped at his sides, and all the lustful power evaporated from his clumsy body. John, drawing up sharply before them, planted a blow on his heaving chest, and he reeled, tottering, on the very brink of the ravine. Then he captured his balance by a desperate effort; stood a moment looking drunk, sick, stupefied; turned, panting, and slunk away.

So John and Ellen were alone-alone with the bridge, and the ravaged Honeymoon Trail, and each other.
